| [stmt-char-sweep-trace](#stmt-char-sweep-trace) | lemma | `char:sweep-trace` | 62 | Let $s=2^\ell$ and let $G_s$ be a sweep using each of the $\ell$ coordinate directions once, in any order, with independent fair switches. |
| [stmt-frames-char-minimum-degree](#stmt-frames-char-minimum-degree) | lemma | `frames:char:minimum-degree` | 63 | For even $n\ge6$, every irreducible representation of $S_n$ other than trivial and sign has degree at least $n/2$.* |
| [stmt-frames-char-block-norm](#stmt-frames-char-block-norm) | proposition | `frames:char:block-norm` | 64 | There is an absolute $C_0$ such that, for every sufficiently large $n=2^d$, every modified block above, and every irreducible $\rho$ of $S_n$ of degree $D$ other than trivial or sign, |
| [stmt-char-main](#stmt-char-main) | theorem | `char:main` | 65 | There are absolute positive integers $C,K$ such that the Thorp shuffle on $n=2^d$ cards has worst-start total-variation distance tending to zero from uniform after $$K\bigl((d-1)+Cd\bigr)$$ physical s |
| [stmt-char-repair](#stmt-char-repair) | lemma | `char:repair` | 66 | Partition a set of size $2s$ into two ordered sets $J_1,J_2$, each of size $s$. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A change of coordinates can relate a prescribed sequence of matching directions to a random one. For a permutation shuffle, we identify the joint path laws under the change of coordinates. A conditional calculation also needs to specify what has been revealed when the next direction is sampled. We separate these two questions. The resulting estimates describe the laws of large ordered lists of cards, and several ways of retaining that information lead to convergence of the whole permutation.

Our model is the Thorp shuffle on $n=2^d$ labeled cards. Split a deck into two equal halves, pair corresponding positions, and interleave the pairs, choosing their orders with independent fair coins. One such operation is one *physical shuffle*. Let $q_d$ be its law as a permutation of positions, let $U_n$ be uniform on $S_n$, and let $\mu*\nu$ denote the law of $XY$ for independent permutations with laws $\mu,\nu$. We use $\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\frac12\sum_g|\mu(g)-\nu(g)|$. A card becomes uniform after $d$ physical shuffles. The cards share the same switches, so the law of their joint positions requires a separate argument.

Our first complete application uses the frame, marginal, degree, and Fourier arguments proved in this paper.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For $n=2^d$, $$\|q_d^{*(32800d)}-U_n\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(d\longrightarrow\infty).$$ The convergence is uniform over every deterministic initial ordering of the deck.*

</div>

The lower obstruction is elementary. After $t$ physical shuffles the process has used $tn/2$ random bits, so its support has size at most $2^{tn/2}$ and $$\|q_d^{*t}-U_n\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ge1-\frac{2^{tn/2}}{n!}.$$ At distance $1/4$ this forces $t\ge\lceil(2/n)\log_2(3n!/4)\rceil=2d-O(1)$. Together with Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, this shows that the mixing time has the optimal order $d=\log_2 n$ on dyadic decks.

The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is independent of the deterministic-coordinate companion, which proves convergence after $1600d$ physical shuffles as $d\to\infty$ <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[optimal-thorp](#ref-optimal-thorp), Theorem 1.1], and of the abstract Fourier transfers in <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier)]. Later we prove that, after $256d$ shuffles, the ordered image of every fixed list of at most $7n/8$ cards has total-variation distance $o(1)$ from a uniform injection, uniformly over the list as $d\to\infty$. The orbit transfer gives the $2048d$ application in Theorem <a href="#shear:thm:2048" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:thm:2048">31</a>. Combining the same marginal estimate with the Fourier companion’s isotypic estimate and its inverse-degree sum gives the $512d$ consequence in Corollary <a href="#shear:cor:512" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:cor:512">30</a>. The orbit argument bounds the operator norm, while the isotypic argument bounds the Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Later sections also prove partial laws that retain information about histories and changing input sets.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Coordinate frames and the first proof

Identify positions with $V=\mathbb F_2^d$. Undoing the deterministic rotation in each shuffle leaves independent fair switches along successive coordinate directions. A run through all $d$ directions is a *sweep*; it costs $d$ physical shuffles. We compare this process with switches in directions $v_1,v_2,\ldots$ such that every $d$ consecutive vectors form a basis. For a fixed sequence whose first basis is the same ordered coordinate basis, invertible changes of position frame identify the joint path laws. At the terminal time, the endpoint laws agree after a specified bijection of output positions. Hence every fixed input list has the same distance from a uniform injection in the two descriptions. A general initial frame also relabels the inputs; the corresponding comparison preserves the average over uniformly chosen input lists.

This path comparison is proved after fixing the complete direction sequence. Its intermediate maps may use future directions. The probability calculation therefore uses a separately generated process: after the first basis, choose the next direction uniformly outside the span of its $d-1$ predecessors, then choose fresh switch coins. The observation field records the input list and any independent initial sampling, the direction prefix, and the path prefixes of the preceding cards in the list. In this field, revealing the next direction does not change the conditional law of the hidden card’s current position.

That card has a conditional mass on the positions not occupied by its predecessors. A switch averages two masses when both endpoints remain available, and transports one mass when only one endpoint remains. The squared deviation from uniform has an exact decrement. Averaging a fresh direction connects every pair across the hyperplane spanned by the preceding directions with the same probability. The expected decrement is at least the current energy times the smaller available half-count, divided by $n$.

For the first proof we sample an omitted block of $n/64$ labels independently of the shuffle. Its image is contained in every available set for the complementary list. Once the shuffle history is fixed, that image is a uniform subset, so both sides of a specified hyperplane contain many of its positions except with exponentially small probability. This is an unconditional imbalance bound after the path conditioning has been removed. Since the energy is at most one, it can be combined with the decrement even when the energy and the imbalance event are correlated. The terminal conditional comparison keeps the complete direction data inside total variation until the frame identity is applied. It yields one partition into 64 blocks for which the sum of the complementary marginal errors after $1025d$ shuffles tends to zero.

Each complementary image list identifies a left coset of the subgroup that permutes the omitted block. A common trimming gives one nearby law whose relevant coset masses are capped by a fixed multiple of their uniform values. On restricting an irreducible representation of dimension $D$ to the product of the 64 block groups, each occurring tensor type has a factor of degree at most $D^{1/64}$. The orbit of one vector under that factor has controlled dimension even when the factor occurs with large multiplicity. The transfer proof first bounds this dimension by a finite sum of squared subgroup degrees. A local estimate for the inverse sixteenth powers then bounds that sum, and Schur averaging turns the coset caps into Fourier contraction. Thirty-two independent time blocks complete the proof, with the sign representation treated separately.

Sections <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a>–<a href="#sec:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:frames">3</a> establish the model and the fixed-schedule path identity. Section <a href="#sec:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:marginals">4</a> proves the marginal estimate. Sections <a href="#sec:lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lift">5</a>, <a href="#sec:degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:degrees">6</a>, and <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">7</a> complete Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> using local representation and Fourier arguments. A reader may stop there with a complete proof.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Further partial laws and their uses

Section <a href="#shear:sec:directions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:sec:directions">8</a> reuses the conditional mass flow with different balance and disclosure statements. A preceding transverse switch gives an imbalance bound for every fixed input list. Independent shears provide an explicit realization of the fresh directions, and the deferred-column calculation identifies the coefficients that have not yet been revealed. Sampling labels offers other balance estimates; an arbitrary initial frame also introduces an input relabeling that disappears after averaging over lists. Thus an average over input lists can enter through the frame comparison or through the balance estimate. Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:delayed-symmetry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:delayed-symmetry">26</a> gives a separate contraction under an earlier conditioning time for the deterministic coordinate schedule.

Section <a href="#frames:chapter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frames:chapter">12</a> controls exceptional trajectories in three different ways. For a chosen partition and chosen orders of its complements, descending removal constructs one retained measure whose complementary marginal probabilities are capped pointwise. A stopped argument uses nested balance events so that the tuple error includes the imbalance probability only once. A different event, defined by the trajectory matchings, supports an entropy inequality for every probability law concentrated on that event. This last quantifier allows the Fourier transfer to condition on a rare event selected from a representation coefficient.

Section <a href="#lifts:random-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lifts:random-marginals">14</a> uses averaged input laws in three ways: selecting one partition for an isotypic estimate, trimming equivariant kernels while controlling their full sign multiplicity spaces, and averaging over overlapping omitted sets. Sections <a href="#frames:char:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frames:char:section">15</a> and <a href="#sec:character" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:character">16</a> use half of the deck. When an independent pass follows a long mixing segment, separate caps on preimage lists control positive trace tests. When the pass comes first, the argument repairs the two image marginals and averages one uniform subgroup coordinate at a time. The repaired coordinates may remain dependent.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### History and related work

The model arose in Thorp’s study of nonrandom shuffling and the game of Faro <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[thorp](#ref-thorp)]. Aldous and Diaconis later described the paired shuffle and reported an informal argument for an $O((\log n)^2)$ threshold <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[aldous-diaconis1987](#ref-aldous-diaconis1987), Section 8b, Example (12)]. For dyadic decks, the description by independent switches along successive hypercube coordinates is standard <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[morris44](#ref-morris44)]. It makes one-card uniformity transparent, while the dependence among card trajectories remains the obstacle to convergence of the full law.

Morris obtained an $O(d^{44})$ bound for the full deck when $n=2^d$, combining chameleon and evolving-set ideas <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[morris44](#ref-morris44)]. Montenegro and Tetali sharpened that analysis to $O(d^{29})$ using spectral-profile and evolving-set estimates <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[montenegro-tetali2006](#ref-montenegro-tetali2006), Theorems 6.14–6.15]. Morris subsequently used entropy estimates to prove $O((\log n)^4)$ for every even deck size <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[morris4](#ref-morris4)] and then $O(d^3)$ for dyadic decks <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[morris3](#ref-morris3)]. These times count individual physical shuffles and concern the entire permutation.

Partial-card laws were studied both as shuffle marginals and as a tool for enciphering small domains. Morris, Rogaway and Stegers gave explicit bounds for ordered Thorp marginals <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[mrs](#ref-mrs), Theorem 1]. For dyadic decks, Czumaj and Vöcking proved that any prescribed list of at most $(1-\epsilon)n$ cards mixes in $O_\epsilon((\log n)^2)$ physical layers, for each fixed $0<\epsilon<1$, using non-Markovian couplings in the butterfly network <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CzumajVocking2014](#ref-CzumajVocking2014)]. Czumaj’s later account identifies this as $O(\log n)$ whole butterflies <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Czumaj2015](#ref-Czumaj2015), Section 1.5]. That paper also obtains logarithmic-depth partial mixing for other switching networks with suitable expansion <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Czumaj2015](#ref-Czumaj2015), Theorems 3.4–3.6].

We adapt the conditional-energy approach in the swap-or-not analysis of Hoang, Morris and Rogaway <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[hmr2014](#ref-hmr2014), Section 3, Theorem 3 and Lemma 4]. Their independent uniform group keys give an unconstrained sequence of matchings; their proof contracts a quadratic discrepancy for the next card and compares the joint law through expected conditional errors. Dai, Hoang and Tessaro later sharpened the combination of those squared discrepancies through a chi-squared inequality <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[dai-hoang-tessaro2017](#ref-dai-hoang-tessaro2017), Section 6]. Here the next direction is constrained by its recent predecessors. The frame identity and the chronological conditional calculation justify that rule and its hyperplane energy estimate.

Related work connects partial information to stronger permutation guarantees in several ways. Ristenpart and Yilek’s mix-and-cut construction, and Morris and Rogaway’s sometimes-recurse construction, use partial or separator guarantees inside recursively modified shuffles <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ristenpart-yilek2013](#ref-ristenpart-yilek2013), [morris-rogaway2014](#ref-morris-rogaway2014)]. Alon and Lovett relate uniformity for every input-output pair of a group action to Fourier matrices of the irreducible representations occurring in that action, and repair sufficiently accurate approximate uniformity to exact uniformity for the action <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[alon-lovett2013](#ref-alon-lovett2013), Theorem 1.4 and Proposition 3.2]. Our passage to the full symmetric group controls its irreducible Fourier matrices from complementary coset caps for selected blocks.

<a id="sec:prelim"></a>
### The chain and its elementary lower bound

Let $d\ge1$ be an integer. Write $n=N=2^d$, let $V=\mathbb F_2^d$, and identify positions $0,\ldots,n-1$ with their binary strings, most significant bit first. Let $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(V)$. Fix card labels indexed by $V$, with card $x$ at position $x$ in the identity state. A state $g\in G$ maps a label to its current position, and permutations are composed as functions: $(gh)(x)=g(h(x))$.

<a id="eq:physical-step"></a>
In one *physical shuffle*, the deck is split into its top and bottom halves, and, for $i=0,\ldots,n/2-1$, the cards at index $i$ within the two halves occupy output positions $2i$ and $2i+1$ in an independently chosen fair order. Thus one step uses $n/2$ independent fair bits. In binary coordinates its action is $$\label{eq:physical-step}
 (x_1,x_2,\ldots,x_d)\longmapsto
 (x_2,\ldots,x_d,x_1+\xi_{(x_2,\ldots,x_d)}),$$ where the bits indexed by the suffix are independent. This convention counts individual physical shuffles, not sweeps of $d$ such shuffles.

<a id="eq:worst-state"></a>
Let $q_d$ be the probability law of the one-step position permutation, and let $U$ be uniform measure on $G$. We also write $U_n$ or $U_G$ when indicating the state-space size or group is useful. The transition kernel is $P_d(g,h)=q_d(hg^{-1})$; thus $P_d^t(g,\cdot)$ is the law after $t$ shuffles started from $g$. For probability measures on a finite set, we use $$\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =\frac12\sum_x|\mu(x)-\nu(x)|
 =\max_A|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|.$$ For measures on $G$, the convolution $\mu*\nu$ is the law of $XY$, where $X\sim\mu$ and $Y\sim\nu$ are independent. Starting from $g_0$, the law after $t$ steps is the right translate of $q_d^{*t}$ by $g_0$. Uniform measure is invariant under every such translation and under left multiplication by every step. Consequently $U$ is stationary, and $$\label{eq:worst-state}
 \max_{g_0\in G}\|P_d^t(g_0,\cdot)-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =\|q_d^{*t}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ Here and below $Y_t$ denotes the original shuffle started at the identity. We define <a id="eq:tmix"></a>$$\label{eq:tmix}
 t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)=
 \min\{t\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}:\|q_d^{*t}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\le1/4\}.$$ Total variation is nonincreasing in $t$ by contraction under convolution. The minimum exists for every $d\ge1$; the coordinate description in Section <a href="#sec:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:frames">3</a> gives a short proof. Our estimates below give the quantitative bound as $d$ grows.

<a id="stmt-lem-lower"></a>
<div id="lem:lower" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sharp-support-time"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Support lower bound). *For every integer $t\ge0$, <a id="eq:exact-support-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-support-lower}
 \|q_d^{*t}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ge 1-\frac{2^{tn/2}}{n!}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:sharp-support-time}
 t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)\ge
 \left\lceil\frac2n\log_2\left(\frac{3n!}{4}\right)\right\rceil
 =2d-O(1).$$ For $0\le t\le d$, $$\|q_d^{*t}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ge1-(e/\sqrt n)^n.$$ In particular, the distance tends to one uniformly for integer $t\le d$, and $t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)\ge d$ for all sufficiently large $d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lower</summary>

*Proof.* There are at most $2^{tn/2}$ choices of all shuffle bits, hence at most that many possible states. If $A$ is their support, then $$\|q_d^{*t}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ge q_d^{*t}(A)-U(A)
 \ge1-\frac{2^{tn/2}}{n!}.$$ If this distance is at most $1/4$, the last ratio must be at least $3/4$. Taking logarithms and then the least integer above the resulting lower bound proves the first part of <a href="#eq:sharp-support-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-support-time">[eq:sharp-support-time]</a>. The integral bound $\log(n!)\ge\int_1^n\log x\,dx=n\log n-n+1$ and $n!\le n^n$ give the asserted asymptotic lower bound. When $t\le d$, the support ratio is at most $n^{n/2}/n!$, which is at most $(e/\sqrt n)^n$. This proves the remaining statements. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-one-card-lower"></a>
<div id="lem:one-card-lower" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (One-card comparison). *For a fixed initial card and an integer $0\le t<d$, its location law has support at most $2^t$. Consequently the full permutation law has total-variation distance at least $1-2^t/n$ from uniform, and $t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)\ge d$. After $d$ steps every individual card has a uniform location.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-one-card-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-one-card-lower</summary>

*Proof.* At each step a card has at most two possible successors. Its support therefore has at most $2^t$ positions. The uniform position law assigns that support mass at most $2^t/n$, and passing from a full permutation to the position of one card cannot increase total variation. For the last assertion, undo the deterministic rotations. During the first $d$ steps each coordinate is refreshed exactly once, with a bit that is fair conditional on the entire previous card path. These successive refreshed bits form a uniform binary string. Restoring the rotation preserves uniformity. ◻

</details>

We shall use basic characteristic-zero representation theory of finite groups: unitary realizations, orthogonal decomposition into irreducibles, Schur’s lemma, and matrix-coefficient orthogonality <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[etingof](#ref-etingof), [sagan](#ref-sagan)]. All representation spaces below are finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces. For an operator $A$, $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ is its operator norm and $\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*A)$ its squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm; the trace is *not* divided by the dimension. We prove explicitly the dimension estimate and the passage from marginals to the full group law that the argument requires.

<a id="sec:frames"></a>
### Changing the position frame

For a nonzero vector $v\in V$, let $C_v\le G$ be the subgroup of permutations that preserve each pair $\{x,x+v\}$ setwise. A uniform element of $C_v$ independently swaps or fixes each of these $n/2$ pairs with equal probability. We call it a *switch in direction $v$*.

<a id="eq:frames:cyclic"></a>
Write $e_1,\ldots,e_d$ for the standard basis of $V$, and let $$R(x_1,\ldots,x_d)=(x_2,\ldots,x_d,x_1)$$ be left cyclic rotation. Binary strings are column vectors when a matrix acts on them. By <a href="#eq:physical-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-step">[eq:physical-step]</a>, one physical shuffle is $RS_t$, where $S_t$ is uniform in $C_{e_1}$ and the $S_t$ are independent. Thus $Y_t=RS_tY_{t-1}$, with $Y_0=\mathrm{id}$. In the rotating position frame, put $$\label{eq:frames:cyclic}
 X_t=R^{-t}Y_t,\qquad
 Q_t=R^{-(t-1)}S_tR^{t-1}.$$ Then $X_t=Q_tX_{t-1}$, and the $Q_t$ are independent uniform switches in the periodic directions $e_1,e_2,\ldots,e_d,e_1,\ldots$. Indeed, conjugation by a linear bijection carries $C_v$ onto the matching subgroup in the image direction, and $R^{-(t-1)}e_1=e_{1+((t-1)\bmod d)}$.

This description also proves the finite-$d$ convergence used in Section <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a>. A sweep of $d$ layers has positive probability of making no swaps, and positive probability of making exactly any chosen coordinate-edge transposition. These transpositions generate $G$, because the cube graph is connected. Every permutation is therefore a product of finitely many sweeps. Padding those products by identity sweeps gives a common number of sweeps at which every permutation has positive probability. That kernel dominates a positive multiple of $U$, so iteration proves convergence. The rotation is the identity at the end of each sweep.

The following path identity allows a different basis in each successive window of $d$ directions. The maps are constructed after the complete schedule is fixed; they need not be known when their time index is reached.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-general-frame"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:general-frame" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Fixed-schedule frame identity). *<span id="lem:frames" label="lem:frames"></span>Let $T\ge d$, and let $\boldsymbol\iota=(\iota_1,\ldots,\iota_d)$ be a permutation of $1,\ldots,d$. Write $i(t)=\iota_{1+((t-1)\bmod d)}$, and let $X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_0=\mathrm{id}$ and $X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_t=Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_t
 X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_{t-1}$, where the increments are independent and uniform in $C_{e_{i(t)}}$.*

<a id="eq:frames:bases"></a>
*Fix directions $v_1,\ldots,v_T$ satisfying $$\label{eq:frames:bases}
 (v_s,\ldots,v_{s+d-1})\text{ is a basis of }V
 \qquad(1\le s\le T-d+1).$$ Let $Z_0=\mathrm{id}$, and let $Z_t$ evolve by independent uniform switches in these directions. There are linear bijections $L_0,\ldots,L_T$, determined by the fixed axis order and direction schedule, such that <a id="shear:eq:general-frame"></a>$$\label{shear:eq:general-frame}
 (Z_t)_{t=0}^T\overset{\mathrm{law}}=
 \bigl(L_tX^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tL_0^{-1}\bigr)_{t=0}^T,
 \qquad L_0e_{\iota_j}=v_j\quad(1\le j\le d).$$ In particular, if $\boldsymbol\iota=(1,\ldots,d)$ and $v_j=e_j$ for $1\le j\le d$, then $L_0=I$ and <a id="eq:frames:endpoint"></a>$$\label{eq:frames:endpoint}
 Z_T\overset{\mathrm{law}}=L_TX_T=L_TR^{-T}Y_T.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-general-frame"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-general-frame</summary>

<a id="eq:frames:recurrence"></a>
*Proof.* For $1\le s\le T-d$, the two successive bases give unique coefficients $a_{s,t}\in\mathbb F_2$, $s<t<s+d$, with $$\label{eq:frames:recurrence}
 v_{s+d}=v_s+\sum_{s<t<s+d}a_{s,t}v_t.$$ The coefficient of $v_s$ cannot vanish, since otherwise $v_{s+1},\ldots,v_{s+d}$ would be dependent; over $\mathbb F_2$ it is therefore one.

<a id="eq:frames:selected-column"></a>
We construct $L_t$ so that its column selected at time $t$ has the required value: $$\label{eq:frames:selected-column}
 L_{t-1}e_{i(t)}=v_t.$$ Begin with the map $L_0$ specified in the statement. For each time $t$, define a row $c_t$ by putting $a_{s,t}$ in column $i(s)$ whenever $1\le s\le T-d$ and $s<t<s+d$, and putting zero in every other column. For fixed $t$ the possible $s$ lie within at most $d-1$ consecutive times, so their coordinate indices are distinct and differ from $i(t)$. Thus $c_te_{i(t)}=0$. Set <a id="eq:frames:matrices"></a>$$\label{eq:frames:matrices}
 L_t=L_{t-1}(I+e_{i(t)}c_t).$$ Each factor squares to $I$ and is invertible. It leaves the selected column unchanged and adds prescribed multiples of that column to the others.

Before a column’s first selection no prescribed update targets it, so <a href="#eq:frames:selected-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:selected-column">[eq:frames:selected-column]</a> holds during the first period by the definition of $L_0$. If it is selected at time $s$ and again at $s+d\le T$, it has value $v_s$ after the first selection. At each intervening time $t$ it receives $a_{s,t}$ times the selected column, whose value is $v_t$ by induction. Equation <a href="#eq:frames:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:recurrence">[eq:frames:recurrence]</a> gives its value $v_{s+d}$ before its next selection. This proves <a href="#eq:frames:selected-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:selected-column">[eq:frames:selected-column]</a>, including an incomplete last period.

<a id="eq:frames:transformed-step"></a>
For this fixed schedule, define the transformed step shown in Figure <a href="#fig:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:frames">1</a>: $$\label{eq:frames:transformed-step}
 \widetilde Q_t
 =L_tQ^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tL_{t-1}^{-1}
 =(L_tL_{t-1}^{-1})
   (L_{t-1}Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tL_{t-1}^{-1}).$$ In <a href="#eq:frames:transformed-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:transformed-step">[eq:frames:transformed-step]</a>, the second factor is uniform in $C_{v_t}$. The first is a fixed member of that subgroup: writing $f_t=c_tL_{t-1}^{-1}$, $$L_tL_{t-1}^{-1}=I+v_tf_t,\qquad f_t(v_t)=0.$$ The map $x\mapsto x+f_t(x)v_t$ fixes or swaps each pair $\{x,x+v_t\}$ because $f_t$ is constant on the pair. Multiplication by it preserves the uniform law on $C_{v_t}$. Each $\widetilde Q_t$ depends on its own independent increment $Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_t$, so the transformed switches are independent. Their products telescope at every time: $$\widetilde Q_t\cdots\widetilde Q_1
 =L_tQ^{\boldsymbol\iota}_t\cdots Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_1L_0^{-1}
 =L_tX^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tL_0^{-1}.$$ This gives the joint path identity, and the cyclic specialization follows from <a href="#eq:frames:cyclic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:cyclic">[eq:frames:cyclic]</a>. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:frames">

<figcaption>One matching switch in two position frames. After the complete schedule is fixed, the vertical maps are deterministic and the lower step is an independent uniform switch in direction $v_{t}$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-frames"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:frames`)

\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, every node/.style={font=\small}]
  \node (a) at (0,1.5) {$x$};
  \node (b) at (6,1.5) {$Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tx$};
  \node (c) at (0,0) {$L_{t-1}x$};
  \node (d) at (6,0) {$L_tQ^{\boldsymbol\iota}_tx$};
  \draw[->] (a) -- node[above] {$Q^{\boldsymbol\iota}_t\in C_{e_{i(t)}}$} (b);
  \draw[->] (a) -- node[left] {$L_{t-1}$} (c);
  \draw[->] (b) -- node[right] {$L_t$} (d);
  \draw[->] (c) -- node[below] {$\widetilde Q_t\in C_{v_t}$} (d);
 \end{tikzpicture}

Top row: node $x$ on the left and node $Q_t^\iota x$ on the right, connected by a rightward horizontal arrow labeled $Q_t^\iota \in C_{e_{i(t)}}$. Bottom row: node $L_{t-1}x$ on the left and node $L_t Q_t^\iota x$ on the right, connected by a rightward horizontal arrow labeled $\widetilde{Q}_t \in C_{v_t}$. <a id="stmt-cor-frames-marginals"></a>
<div id="cor:frames:marginals" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:frames:tuple-input-map"></a>
**Corollary 5** (Endpoint distances and the input map). *Under Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a>, let $0\le q\le n$ and let $\pi_q$ be uniform on ordered distinct $q$-tuples of positions. For every fixed ordered list $C$ of $q$ distinct labels, $$\label{eq:frames:tuple-input-map}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C))-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =
 \|\mathcal L(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T(L_0^{-1}C))-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ Thus the same-list distances agree when $L_0=I$. Their averages also agree when $C$ is sampled uniformly over ordered distinct lists, independently of the switch process: <a id="eq:frames:averaged-input-map"></a>$$\label{eq:frames:averaged-input-map}
 \mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =
 \mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T(C)\mid C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-frames-marginals"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-frames-marginals</summary>

*Proof.* The terminal map $L_T$ is a bijection on distinct position tuples and preserves $\pi_q$, which proves <a href="#eq:frames:tuple-input-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:tuple-input-map">[eq:frames:tuple-input-map]</a>. The input map $L_0^{-1}$ also preserves the uniform law on distinct lists, giving the averaged identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-frames-conditioning"></a>
<div id="rem:frames:conditioning" class="remark">

*Remark 6* (The schedule is fixed before the frame is constructed). If a random schedule satisfying <a href="#eq:frames:bases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:bases">[eq:frames:bases]</a> is sampled independently of the switch randomness, the lemma and corollary apply conditionally on each complete schedule. The averaged identity then uses a list sampled independently of that schedule and of the switches. The maps $L_t$, including $L_0$ when the first basis is random, may depend on directions that have not yet been revealed. We use them only in these conditional path and endpoint comparisons. The energy calculation next uses a separately generated process with directions revealed in chronological order.

</div>

<a id="sec:marginals"></a>
### Online observations and ordinary marginals

We track the conditional location of one hidden card while revealing the paths of earlier cards in a list. The quadratic error and the successive comparison of conditional location laws follow the swap-or-not approach of Hoang, Morris, and Rogaway <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[hmr2014](#ref-hmr2014), Section 3, Theorem 3 and Lemma 4]. Here the next direction is constrained by its recent predecessors, so we state the information used in the calculation explicitly. The first application below produces one partition whose complementary marginals are all close to uniform. Later applications use the same calculation with other sources of balance.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### A chronological law and a hidden card

Fix an integer $T\ge0$. Let $\mathbf v=(v_1,\ldots,v_T)$ be a random schedule of nonzero directions. Set $Z_0=\mathrm{id}$, and suppose that, conditional on the complete schedule, the increments of $Z$ are independent uniform switches in directions $v_1,\ldots,v_T$. This law can be generated in time order: sample the next direction from its conditional law given the preceding directions, and then use fresh fair coins on its matching.

<a id="eq:marginal-filtration"></a>
Let $\Xi$ be initial data independent of the joint process $(\mathbf v,(Z_t)_{t=0}^T)$, and let $C=(c_1,\ldots,c_q)$ be a list of distinct labels determined by $\Xi$, with fixed length $0\le q\le n$. A fixed list corresponds to deterministic $\Xi$; a sampled partition may be included in $\Xi$ when it determines the list. For its $j$th label define the exact online field $$\label{eq:marginal-filtration}
 \mathcal F_t^{j}
 =\sigma\bigl(\Xi,v_1,\ldots,v_t,\,
        Z_s(c_i):0\le s\le t,\ i<j\bigr).$$ Let $D_t^j$ be the positions not occupied by the observed labels $c_1,\ldots,c_{j-1}$ at time $t$, and put $r_j=n-j+1$. Define <a id="eq:marginal-energy"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-energy}
 p_t^j(x)=\Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x\mid\mathcal F_t^j\},\qquad
 \Delta_t^j=\sum_{x\in D_t^j}\left(p_t^j(x)-\frac1{r_j}\right)^2.$$ We extend $p_t^j$ by zero outside $D_t^j$. It is a probability vector on that set, so $0\le\Delta_t^j=\sum_xp_t^j(x)^2-1/r_j\le1$.

<a id="eq:marginal-direction-factorization"></a>
For $0\le t<T$ and a feasible direction prefix write $K_t(v)=\Pr\{v_{t+1}=v\mid v_1,\ldots,v_t\}$. Conditional on the complete schedule, the law of the *joint* permutation path through time $t$ depends only on $v_1,\ldots,v_t$. The independence of $\Xi$ therefore gives $$\label{eq:marginal-direction-factorization}
 \Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x,\ v_{t+1}=v\mid\mathcal F_t^j\}
 =p_t^j(x)K_t(v).$$ Indeed, the joint law of the old hidden position and observed path prefix is the same for every completion of a fixed direction prefix. Conditioning on that observed prefix leaves the conditional law of the future directions unchanged. This argument uses only path prefixes; it does not condition on future observed paths or on further auxiliary data.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-energy"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:marginal-cross-energy"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Online averaging and cross-hyperplane contraction). *<span id="lem:marginal-averaging" label="lem:marginal-averaging"></span>In the preceding setup, for $0\le t<T$ and $1\le j\le q$, <a id="eq:marginal-exact-decrement"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-exact-decrement}
 \Delta_{t+1}^j=\Delta_t^j-\frac12
 \sum_{\substack{\{x,y\}\subseteq D_t^j\\y=x+v_{t+1}}}
       \bigl(p_t^j(x)-p_t^j(y)\bigr)^2
 \quad\text{almost surely},$$ where each unordered matching edge is counted once. If $K_t$ is uniform on the complement of an $\mathcal F_t^j$-measurable hyperplane $J_t$, then $$\label{eq:marginal-cross-energy}
 \mathbb E[\Delta_t^j-\Delta_{t+1}^j\mid\mathcal F_t^j]
 \ge
 \frac{\min(|D_t^j\cap J_t|,|D_t^j\setminus J_t|)}{n}\Delta_t^j.$$ More generally, suppose $K_t$ is uniform outside an $\mathcal F_t^j$-measurable proper subspace $W_t$, and let $J_t$ be an $\mathcal F_t^j$-measurable hyperplane containing $W_t$. Then the same right side with denominator $2n$ is a valid lower bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-energy</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $j$ and omit its superscript. By <a href="#eq:marginal-direction-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-direction-factorization">[eq:marginal-direction-factorization]</a>, revealing $v_{t+1}$ does not update the old hidden-card law $p_t$. Given $\mathcal F_t$ and this direction, the matching edges incident to observed cards are known. Their next positions reveal exactly the fresh coins on those edges. Those coins are independent of the old hidden position, so their revelation also leaves $p_t$ unchanged; all other edge coins remain independent and fair.

An edge containing two positions of $D_t$ averages their two masses. An edge containing exactly one position of $D_t$ transports its mass to the unique position on that edge unoccupied by observed cards after the switch. Edges with no available position carry no hidden mass. These rules give the conditional vector at the exact field $\mathcal F_{t+1}$ in <a href="#eq:marginal-filtration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-filtration">[eq:marginal-filtration]</a>. They also transport the uniform vector on $D_t$ to the uniform vector on $D_{t+1}$. Transport preserves squared error, while averaging two masses reduces it by $\frac12(p_t(x)-p_t(y))^2$. This proves the exact identity.

<a id="shear:eq:bipartite-form"></a>
For the expectation, split $D_t=D^0\cup D^1$ across $J_t$ and put $z_x=p_t(x)-1/r_j$, $u=|D^0|$, and $w=|D^1|$. Since $\sum z_x=0$, $$\label{shear:eq:bipartite-form}
 \sum_{x\in D^0,y\in D^1}(z_x-z_y)^2
 =w\sum_{D^0}z_x^2+u\sum_{D^1}z_y^2
     +2\left(\sum_{D^0}z_x\right)^2
 \ge\min(u,w)\sum_{D_t}z_x^2.$$ Every cross pair has matching probability $2/n$ in the hyperplane case. Combining this with the factor $1/2$ in the exact decrement proves <a href="#eq:marginal-cross-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-cross-energy">[eq:marginal-cross-energy]</a>. In the proper-subspace case, every cross difference lies outside $W_t$ and has probability $1/(n-|W_t|)\ge1/n$; discard the other eligible pairs to obtain the denominator $2n$. The calculation covers an empty side. The algebraic identity <a href="#shear:eq:bipartite-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:bipartite-form">[shear:eq:bipartite-form]</a> holds for any real centered vector; a later use for another vector must also identify its averaging rule. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:marginal-balance-recurrence"></a>
The loss bound and a balance estimate will be used at different levels of conditioning. Let $0\le\gamma\le1$. If the conditional expected loss $\mathbb E[\Delta_t^j-\Delta_{t+1}^j\mid\mathcal F_t^j]$ is at least $\gamma\Delta_t^j$ off an event $B_t\in\mathcal F_t^j$ and is always nonnegative, then $\Delta_t^j\le1$ gives $$\label{eq:marginal-balance-recurrence}
 \mathbb E\Delta_{t+1}^j
 \le(1-\gamma)\mathbb E\Delta_t^j+\gamma\Pr(B_t).$$ Thus an unconditional bound on $\Pr(B_t)$ suffices; no bound conditional on the observed paths is asserted.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### From hidden cards to an ordinary tuple law

<a id="eq:marginal-sequential-tv"></a>
Let $\pi_q$ be uniform on ordered distinct $q$-tuples of positions, and let $U_D$ be uniform on a nonempty set $D$. For any tuple law $\nu=\mathcal L(Y_1,\ldots,Y_q)$, $$\label{eq:marginal-sequential-tv}
 \|\nu-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\sum_{j=1}^q\mathbb E_\nu
 \left\|
 \mathcal L(Y_j\mid Y_1,\ldots,Y_{j-1})
 -U_{V\setminus\{Y_1,\ldots,Y_{j-1}\}}
 \right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ This sequential comparison appears in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[mrs](#ref-mrs), Appendix A, Lemma 2]; an earlier distinct-tuple form is <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[morris-exclusion2006](#ref-morris-exclusion2006), Section 5.3, Lemma 12]. For completeness, at the $j$th step insert the law that first draws the actual $(j-1)$-coordinate prefix of $\nu$ and then appends a uniform available position. Its distance from the $j$-coordinate marginal of $\nu$ is the $j$th summand above. Applying the same extension kernel to $\pi_{j-1}$ gives $\pi_j$ and contracts total variation. The triangle inequality and induction prove <a href="#eq:marginal-sequential-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-sequential-tv">[eq:marginal-sequential-tv]</a>. Every expectation is under the actual prefix law.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-tuple"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:tuple" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:marginal-terminal-identification"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:tuple-bound"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Terminal data and an ordinary tuple law). *In the preceding setup let $\Lambda$ be terminal data that determine the complete direction schedule, and suppose $\Xi$ is independent of $(\Lambda,(Z_t)_{t=0}^T)$. For every $1\le j\le q$ and every $x\in V$, assume that its online terminal vector is also the conditional hidden law after these data and the complete predecessor paths are known: $$\label{eq:marginal-terminal-identification}
 \Pr\{Z_T(c_j)=x\mid
       \Lambda,\Xi,\,
       Z_s(c_i):0\le s\le T,\ i<j\}
 =p_T^j(x)\quad\text{almost surely}.$$ Then $$\label{shear:eq:tuple-bound}
 \mathbb E_{\Lambda,\Xi}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\Lambda,\Xi)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac12\sum_{j=1}^q
       \sqrt{(n-j+1)\mathbb E\Delta_T^j}.$$ All expectations are under the actual joint law of the data, list, and process. If $\mathbb E\Delta_T^j\le a_T$ for every $j$, the right side is at most $q\sqrt n\,\sqrt{a_T}/2$. For $q=0$ both sides are zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-tuple"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-tuple</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $\Lambda,\Xi$, apply <a href="#eq:marginal-sequential-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-sequential-tv">[eq:marginal-sequential-tv]</a>. At index $j$ use the coarser field $$\mathcal H_j
 =\sigma(\Lambda,\Xi,Z_T(c_1),\ldots,Z_T(c_{j-1})).$$ The tower property and <a href="#eq:marginal-terminal-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-terminal-identification">[eq:marginal-terminal-identification]</a> give $\Pr\{Z_T(c_j)=x\mid\mathcal H_j\}
 =\mathbb E[p_T^j(x)\mid\mathcal H_j]$. The set $D_T^j$ is $\mathcal H_j$-measurable, so Jensen’s inequality for the $\ell^1$ norm compares both vectors to the same uniform law $U_{D_T^j}$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\|p_T^j-U_{D_T^j}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac12\sqrt{(n-j+1)\Delta_T^j}.$$ Consequently, almost surely in the terminal data and initial data, <a id="eq:marginal-conditional-tuple"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-conditional-tuple}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\Lambda,\Xi)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac12\sum_{j=1}^q
 \mathbb E\!\left[\sqrt{(n-j+1)\Delta_T^j}\,\middle|\,\Lambda,\Xi\right].$$ Averaging and applying Cauchy–Schwarz once more proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

When $\Lambda=\mathbf v$, the field on the left of <a href="#eq:marginal-terminal-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-terminal-identification">[eq:marginal-terminal-identification]</a> is exactly $\mathcal F_T^j$, so that hypothesis is automatic. If $\Lambda$ contains further shear or functional data, the identification requires an additional path-law argument. We will prove that argument where those data are introduced; their disclosure is separate from the chronological averaging above.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A sampled omitted block

For the local full-group proof, set <a id="eq:marginal-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-constants}
 k=64,\qquad m=\frac{n}{k},\qquad
 \alpha=\frac1{4k},\qquad T=(1+16k)d=1025d,$$ and assume $d\ge6$. Put <a id="eq:marginal-error"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-error}
 b_n=2\left(\frac{2}{3^{3/4}}\right)^m,\qquad
 \varepsilon_n=\frac{n^{3/2}}2\sqrt{e^{-4d}+b_n}.$$ For a labeled partition $\mathcal M=(M_1,\ldots,M_k)$ of $V$ into $m$-element blocks, let $\delta_a(\mathcal M)$ be the distance of the time-$T$ images of $V\setminus M_a$ under the physical shuffle from the uniform distinct tuple law. Any fixed ordering of these labels gives the same distance.

<a id="stmt-prop-marginals"></a>
<div id="prop:marginals" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (One partition with nearly complete marginals). *There is a deterministic labeled partition $\mathcal M=(M_1,\ldots,M_k)$ into blocks of size $m$ for which <a id="eq:marginal-partition"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-partition}
 \sum_{a=1}^k\delta_a(\mathcal M)
 \le k\varepsilon_n=o(1)\qquad(d\longrightarrow\infty).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-marginals"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-marginals</summary>

*Proof.* Use $v_i=e_i$ for $1\le i\le d$. For $d\le t<T$ put <a id="eq:marginal-hyperplane"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-hyperplane}
 J_t=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{v_{t-d+2},\ldots,v_t\}$$ and choose $v_{t+1}$ uniformly in $V\setminus J_t$, with fresh direction randomness. Every $d$ successive directions form a basis. Conditional on the complete schedule, use independent fair switches to form $Z$. Independently choose $\mathcal M$ uniformly among labeled equal-size partitions. For a fixed $a$, let $\Xi$ be this partition and let $C$ be any deterministic ordering of $V\setminus M_a$, with $q=n-m$. This is the online setup above.

For each hidden index $j$, the available set contains the positions of all omitted labels: <a id="eq:marginal-permanent-holes"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-permanent-holes}
 Z_t(M_a)\subseteq D_t^j.$$ Write $D_t^{j,0}=D_t^j\cap J_t$ and $D_t^{j,1}=D_t^j\setminus J_t$. If $J_t^0=J_t$ and $J_t^1=V\setminus J_t$, then $$\left\{\min_i|D_t^j\cap J_t^i|<m/4\right\}
 \subseteq
 \left\{\min_i|Z_t(M_a)\cap J_t^i|<m/4\right\}.$$ Fix the direction and switch history through time $t$, but not $\mathcal M$. This fixes $Z_t$ and $J_t$, while $Z_t(M_a)$ is a uniform $m$-subset of $V$.

<a id="eq:marginal-balance"></a>
For a uniform $m$-subset and a fixed half of $V$, let $X$ be its count in that half. View the subset as an ordered sample without replacement. A specified set of $\ell$ sampled indices all lie in the half with probability at most $2^{-\ell}$. If $I_i$ are the membership indicators, expansion gives $$\mathbb E3^X=\mathbb E\prod_{i=1}^m(1+2I_i)
 \le\sum_{A\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}}2^{|A|}2^{-|A|}=2^m.$$ If one half contains fewer than $m/4$ sampled points, the other contains more than $3m/4$. Markov’s inequality and a union bound therefore yield, for $d\le t<T$, $$\label{eq:marginal-balance}
 \Pr\{\min(|D_t^{j,0}|,|D_t^{j,1}|)<m/4\}
 \le2\frac{2^m}{3^{3m/4}}=b_n.$$ This bound averages the independent partition and the shuffle; it is not a concentration assertion conditional on the observed paths.

<a id="eq:marginal-terminal-energy"></a>
Off the event in <a href="#eq:marginal-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-balance">[eq:marginal-balance]</a>, the conditional loss in <a href="#eq:marginal-cross-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-cross-energy">[eq:marginal-cross-energy]</a> is at least $\alpha\Delta_t^j$. Its loss is always nonnegative. Applying <a href="#eq:marginal-balance-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-balance-recurrence">[eq:marginal-balance-recurrence]</a> gives <a id="eq:marginal-recursion"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-recursion}
 \mathbb E\Delta_{t+1}^j
 \le(1-\alpha)\mathbb E\Delta_t^j+\alpha b_n.$$ Starting with $\Delta_d^j\le1$ and iterating for $T-d=16kd$ steps, we obtain, uniformly in $a,j$, $$\label{eq:marginal-terminal-energy}
 \mathbb E\Delta_T^j\le(1-\alpha)^{T-d}+b_n
 \le e^{-4d}+b_n.$$

<a id="eq:marginal-averaged-tuple"></a>
Let $\widetilde\delta_a(\mathbf v,\mathcal M)$ be the auxiliary tuple distance conditional on the complete schedule and partition. Use Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a> with $\Lambda=\mathbf v$. Its terminal identification is automatic, since $\mathcal F_T^j$ already contains the complete schedule and predecessor paths. Equations <a href="#shear:eq:tuple-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:tuple-bound">[shear:eq:tuple-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:marginal-terminal-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-terminal-energy">[eq:marginal-terminal-energy]</a> give $$\label{eq:marginal-averaged-tuple}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf v,\mathcal M}
 \widetilde\delta_a(\mathbf v,\mathcal M)
 \le\frac{n^{3/2}}2\sqrt{e^{-4d}+b_n}
 =\varepsilon_n.$$ The schedule remains inside the distance on the left.

For every fixed schedule and partition, choose $\boldsymbol\iota=(1,\ldots,d)$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a>. The first directions are $v_j=e_j$, so $L_0=I$. Corollary <a href="#cor:frames:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:frames:marginals">5</a>, followed by the terminal rotation, therefore gives the same-list identity <a id="eq:marginal-frame-transfer"></a>$$\label{eq:marginal-frame-transfer}
 \widetilde\delta_a(\mathbf v,\mathcal M)=\delta_a(\mathcal M).$$ The frame maps enter only this fixed-schedule endpoint comparison. Summing <a href="#eq:marginal-averaged-tuple" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-averaged-tuple">[eq:marginal-averaged-tuple]</a> over $a$ now gives $\mathbb E_{\mathcal M}\sum_a\delta_a(\mathcal M)\le k\varepsilon_n$. At least one deterministic partition attains this average bound. Finally, $n^{3/2}e^{-2d}=\exp((\tfrac32\log2-2)d)\to0$, while $2/3^{3/4}<1$ makes $b_n$ exponentially small in $n$. Thus $\varepsilon_n\to0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:lift"></a>
### From large marginals to a finite degree sum

Proposition <a href="#prop:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marginals">9</a> gives one partition for which the complementary image laws have small total error. We first express those image laws as coset laws and condition on one event that bounds all their coset densities. We then turn these bounds into a finite sum of squared degrees of representations of the block groups. The next section will estimate that sum by counting tableaux.

Throughout this section, $U_G$ denotes the uniform probability measure on a finite group $G$. For a subgroup $H$, we use the standard convention that $gH$ is a *left coset*. Its elements are obtained from $g$ by multiplication on the right by elements of $H$. If $\mu$ is a probability measure on $G$, write $\mu_H$ for its pushforward to the set $G/H$ of left cosets. Thus $$\mu_H(gH)=\mu(gH),
  \qquad (U_G)_H(gH)=\frac{|H|}{|G|}.$$

For the shuffle application, take $X=V$, set $T=1025d$ and $\mu=q_d^{*T}$, and use the deterministic partition $M_1,\ldots,M_k$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marginals">9</a>, with $k=64$ and $|M_a|=n/k$. Let $H_a$ permute $M_a$ and fix $X\setminus M_a$ pointwise. A permutation maps each reference label to its current position. Two maps $g,g'$ agree on $X\setminus M_a$ exactly when $g^{-1}g'\in H_a$, or equivalently $g'\in gH_a$. Thus the ordered images of the cards outside $M_a$ identify the coset $gH_a$ bijectively. Uniform measure on permutations induces uniform measure on these ordered distinct images. The marginal distances in Proposition <a href="#prop:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marginals">9</a> are consequently exactly the coset distances $\delta_a$ for the trimming lemma below, and $\sum_a\delta_a=o(1)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-trim"></a>
<div id="lem:trim" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lift:trim"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Simultaneous trimming). *Let $H_1,\ldots,H_k$ be subgroups of a finite group $G$, and let $\mu$ be a probability measure on $G$. Put $$\delta_a=\|\mu_{H_a}-(U_G)_{H_a}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},
  \qquad \delta=\sum_{a=1}^k\delta_a.$$ If $\delta<1/2$, there is a set $E\subseteq G$, of removed mass $\varepsilon=\mu(G\setminus E)\le 2\delta$, such that the conditional probability measure $\bar\mu=\mu(\,\cdot\mid E)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:lift:trim}
  \|\bar\mu-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\varepsilon,
  \qquad
  \bar\mu(gH_a)
  \le \frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}\frac{|H_a|}{|G|}
  \quad(g\in G,\ 1\le a\le k).$$ In particular, if $\delta\le 1/4$, the last density bound is at most $4|H_a|/|G|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trim"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trim</summary>

*Proof.* For each $a$, call a coset $C\in G/H_a$ bad if $\mu(C)>2U_G(C)$, and let $B_a$ be the union of its bad cosets. The identity expressing total variation as the sum of positive excesses gives $$\mu(B_a)
  \le 2\sum_{\substack{C\in G/H_a\\ \mu(C)>2U_G(C)}}
                 \bigl(\mu(C)-U_G(C)\bigr)
  \le 2\delta_a.$$ Take $E=G\setminus\bigcup_a B_a$. The union bound yields $\varepsilon\le 2\delta<1$, so conditioning is well defined. On $E$, the mass of $\bar\mu$ exceeds that of $\mu$ by total amount $\varepsilon$; on the complement, its mass is zero. This proves the total-variation equality in <a href="#eq:lift:trim" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:trim">[eq:lift:trim]</a>.

Fix a coset $C\in G/H_a$. If it is bad for this subgroup, then $C\cap E=\varnothing$. Otherwise, $$\bar\mu(C)
  =\frac{\mu(C\cap E)}{1-\varepsilon}
  \le \frac{\mu(C)}{1-\varepsilon}
  \le \frac{2U_G(C)}{1-\varepsilon}.$$ The same estimate therefore holds for every coset in every one of the $k$ systems. If $\delta\le 1/4$, then $1-\varepsilon\ge 1/2$, giving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

We now convert the coset bounds into control of averages of representation matrices. For a mass function $a:G\to\mathbb C$ and a unitary representation $\rho$, define its Fourier matrix by $$\widehat a(\rho)=\sum_{g\in G}a(g)\rho(g).$$ Probability and subprobability measures are identified with their mass functions. For a finite group $H$, write $\widehat H$ for the set of equivalence classes of its complex irreducible representations.

When an irreducible representation of $G$ is restricted to the product of the block groups, each irreducible summand is a tensor product of one representation from each group. If the full representation has dimension $D$, each such tensor product has dimension at most $D$, so at least one factor has degree at most $D^{1/k}$. The following proposition measures the cost of these small factors by a finite sum. Its proof shows why each equivalence class of a subgroup representation contributes only the square of its degree even when it occurs with large multiplicity.

<a id="stmt-prop-lift-orbit-sum"></a>
<div id="prop:lift:orbit-sum" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:lift:coset-cap"></a>
<a id="eq:lift:orbit-sum"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Coset densities and squared subgroup degrees). *Let a finite set $X$ be partitioned into $k\ge1$ nonempty sets $M_1,\ldots,M_k$, and put $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(X)$. Let $H_a$ be the subgroup permuting $M_a$ and fixing $X\setminus M_a$ pointwise. Suppose that a probability measure $\nu$ on $G$ satisfies, for some $B\ge 1$, $$\label{eq:lift:coset-cap}
  \nu(gH_a)\le B\frac{|H_a|}{|G|}
  \qquad(g\in G,\ 1\le a\le k).$$ Then every irreducible complex unitary representation $\rho$ of $G$, of dimension $D$, satisfies $$\label{eq:lift:orbit-sum}
  \|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
  \le \frac{B}{D}\sum_{a=1}^k
       \sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H_a\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}
                    (\dim\pi)^2.$$ The block sizes need not be equal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-lift-orbit-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-lift-orbit-sum</summary>

<a id="eq:lift:restriction"></a>
*Proof.* Because the blocks are disjoint, their subgroups form the direct product $H=H_1\times\cdots\times H_k$ inside $G$. By complete reducibility and the irreducible-representation theorem for finite direct products, the restriction of $\rho$ to $H$ is an orthogonal sum of isotypic spaces of the form $$\label{eq:lift:restriction}
  (V_{\pi_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\pi_k})
       \otimes \mathcal A_{\boldsymbol\pi}.$$ Here $\pi_a$ is an irreducible representation of $H_a$, and $\mathcal A_{\boldsymbol\pi}$ is a multiplicity space on which $H$ acts trivially. These are the usual characteristic-zero representation facts; see, for example, <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[sagan](#ref-sagan), [etingof](#ref-etingof)].

For every type $\boldsymbol\pi$ that occurs, its tensor-product dimension is at most $D$. Consequently some index $a$ satisfies $\dim\pi_a\le D^{1/k}$. Assign each entire isotypic space in <a href="#eq:lift:restriction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:restriction">[eq:lift:restriction]</a> to one such index, choosing the least one in case of a tie. Grouping the assigned spaces yields an orthogonal decomposition $$V_\rho=E_1\oplus\cdots\oplus E_k.$$ Each $E_a$ is invariant under $H$, and every irreducible of $H_a$ appearing in $E_a$ has dimension at most $D^{1/k}$. The multiplicities in these restrictions are unrestricted.

<a id="eq:lift:orbit-dimension"></a>
For this fixed representation, put $$r_a=\sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H_a\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}
                (\dim\pi)^2.$$ Fix $u_a\in E_a$, and set $$W_a=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{\rho(h)u_a:h\in H_a\}.$$ We first bound this single-vector orbit span. Regroup all copies of each $H_a$-irreducible, including copies coming from different $H$-types, into a single $H_a$-isotypic space. In such an isotypic space $V_\pi\otimes\mathcal A_\pi$, with $b=\dim\pi$, the group acts by $\pi(h)\otimes\mathrm{id}$. If $v$ is the projection of $u_a$ to this space, its orbit span is contained in the image of the linear map $$\operatorname{End}(V_\pi)\longrightarrow
          V_\pi\otimes\mathcal A_\pi,
  \qquad Q\longmapsto (Q\otimes\mathrm{id})v.$$ That image has dimension at most $b^2$, independently of $\dim\mathcal A_\pi$. Each inequivalent $H_a$-type contributes only once. Summing over the different isotypic spaces gives the finite bound $$\dim W_a\le r_a
  =\sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H_a\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}
                         (\dim\pi)^2.
\label{eq:lift:orbit-dimension}$$

<a id="eq:lift:schur-average"></a>
Let $\Pi_a$ be the orthogonal projection onto $W_a$. The subspace $W_a$ is $H_a$-invariant, and therefore $\rho(gh)W_a=\rho(g)W_a$ for $h\in H_a$. For each vector $z$, the nonnegative function $$g\longmapsto
       \|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_a}z\|^2$$ is thus constant on each left coset $gH_a$. The coset bound <a href="#eq:lift:coset-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:coset-cap">[eq:lift:coset-cap]</a> implies <a id="eq:lift:projection-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:lift:projection-comparison}
  \mathbb E_{g\sim\nu}\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_a}z\|^2
  \le B\mathbb E_{g\sim U_G}\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_a}z\|^2.$$ The uniform average of these projections is $$Q_a=\frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\rho(g)\Pi_a\rho(g)^*.$$ It commutes with every $\rho(g)$. Schur’s lemma makes it a scalar multiple of $\mathrm{id}$, and its trace is $\dim W_a$. Hence $$\label{eq:lift:schur-average}
  Q_a=\frac{\dim W_a}{D}\mathrm{id},
  \qquad
  \mathbb E_{g\sim U_G}\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_a}z\|^2
       =\frac{\dim W_a}{D}\|z\|^2.$$

<a id="eq:lift:component-bound"></a>
Since $\rho(g)u_a\in\rho(g)W_a$, Cauchy–Schwarz, first for expectation and then in the representation space, gives $$\begin{aligned}
  |\langle z,\widehat\nu(\rho)u_a\rangle|^2
  &\le \mathbb E_{g\sim\nu}|\langle z,\rho(g)u_a\rangle|^2 \notag\\
  &\le \|u_a\|^2
           \mathbb E_{g\sim\nu}\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_a}z\|^2 \notag\\
  &\le \frac{Br_a}{D}\|u_a\|^2\|z\|^2,
\label{eq:lift:component-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ where the last line uses <a href="#eq:lift:orbit-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:orbit-dimension">[eq:lift:orbit-dimension]</a>–<a href="#eq:lift:schur-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:schur-average">[eq:lift:schur-average]</a>. For an arbitrary $u=\sum_a u_a$, sum the square-root form of this estimate and apply Cauchy–Schwarz in the index $a$: $$\begin{aligned}
 |\langle z,\widehat\nu(\rho)u\rangle|
 &\le \sqrt{\frac{B}{D}}\,\|z\|
           \sum_{a=1}^k\sqrt{r_a}\,\|u_a\|\\
 &\le \sqrt{\frac{B}{D}\sum_{a=1}^k r_a}\,\|z\|
           \left(\sum_{a=1}^k\|u_a\|^2\right)^{1/2}\\
 &=\sqrt{\frac{B}{D}\sum_{a=1}^k r_a}\,\|z\|\,\|u\|.
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality uses the orthogonality of the $E_a$. Taking the supremum over unit vectors $u,z$ and squaring proves <a href="#eq:lift:orbit-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:orbit-sum">[eq:lift:orbit-sum]</a>. The argument includes zero components and one-dimensional representations; in dimension one the displayed sum does not provide decay. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:degrees"></a>
### An elementary bound on representation degrees

<a id="eq:degrees:truncated-sum"></a>
Proposition <a href="#prop:lift:orbit-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift:orbit-sum">11</a> reduced the Fourier estimate to a finite sum of squared degrees of small representations of each block group. We now bound that sum. For integers $s,D,k\ge1$ and a group $H\cong S_s$, we have $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}(\dim\pi)^2
 &=\sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}
       (\dim\pi)^{18}(\dim\pi)^{-16}\notag\\
 &\le D^{18/k}\sum_{\pi\in\widehat H}(\dim\pi)^{-16}.
\label{eq:degrees:truncated-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus a uniform bound on the sum of inverse sixteenth powers of degrees will control every block size in the preceding proposition. We also prove that the sum tends to zero after the trivial and sign representations are removed. That second statement will control the full-group Fourier sum in the completion.

Recall that the complex irreducible representations of $S_s$ are indexed by partitions $\lambda\vdash s$, and that their degrees $f^\lambda$ count standard Young tableaux of shape $\lambda$. Such a tableau fills the diagram with $1,\ldots,s$, increasing along every row and column. The partitions $(s)$ and $(1^s)$ afford the trivial and sign representations, respectively; transposing a tableau gives $f^{\lambda'}=f^\lambda$. These are the classical Specht-module and standard-tableau facts <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[sagan](#ref-sagan), [etingof](#ref-etingof)]. Liebeck and Shalev prove the stronger estimate $\sum_{\lambda\vdash s}(f^\lambda)^{-u}=2+O_u(s^{-u})$ as $s\to\infty$, for every fixed $u>0$ <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[liebeck-shalev2004](#ref-liebeck-shalev2004), Theorems 1.1 and 2.6]. The following elementary proof at exponent $16$ keeps this application independent of that stronger estimate; it uses only the tableau facts just recalled.

<a id="stmt-lem-degrees"></a>
<div id="lem:degrees" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:degrees:uniform"></a>
<a id="eq:degrees:vanishing"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Summability of inverse degrees). *There is an absolute finite constant $$C_0:=\sup_{s\ge 1}\sum_{\lambda\vdash s}(f^\lambda)^{-16}<\infty.
  \label{eq:degrees:uniform}$$ Moreover, $$\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash s\\
                  \lambda\notin\{(s),(1^s)\}}}
       (f^\lambda)^{-16}\longrightarrow 0
       \qquad\text{as }s\longrightarrow\infty.
  \label{eq:degrees:vanishing}$$ The excluded partitions are interpreted as a set, so when $s=1$ only the single partition $(1)$ is excluded.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-degrees"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-degrees</summary>

<a id="eq:degrees:tableau-construction"></a>
*Proof.* We first record two elementary counting observations. If a diagram has first row of length $a$ and exactly $b$ boxes below it, where $0\le b\le a$, then $$f^\lambda\ge \binom{a}{b}.
  \label{eq:degrees:tableau-construction}$$ This is the tableau construction of Liebeck and Shalev <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[liebeck-shalev2004](#ref-liebeck-shalev2004), Lemma 2.1], with their parameters $n=a+b$ and $k=b$. To see it directly, place $1,\ldots,b$ in the first $b$ boxes of the first row. Choose any $b$ labels from $\{b+1,\ldots,a+b\}$ for the boxes below that row, and put them in increasing row-major order: read rows from top to bottom, and each row from left to right. Fill the rest of the first row increasingly with the remaining labels. Every box below the first row lies in one of its first $b$ columns. Its top-row predecessor therefore has a label at most $b$, while all lower labels are larger than $b$. Row-major filling respects both row and column inequalities within the lower diagram, so this is a standard tableau. Different choices of lower labels give different tableaux. When $b=0$ the construction is the unique tableau of a single row.

<a id="eq:degrees:extension"></a>
Also, if $\mu$ is a Young subdiagram of $\lambda$, every standard tableau of $\mu$ extends to one of $\lambda$: retain its labels and fill the remaining boxes in row-major order with the successive larger labels. Because a Young subdiagram is closed under moving up and left, all required predecessors have already been filled. Consequently, $$f^\lambda\ge f^\mu.
  \label{eq:degrees:extension}$$

<a id="eq:degrees:partition-count"></a>
Write $p(t)$ for the number of partitions of $t$. For $t\ge 1$, regarding a partition as an ordered composition gives $$p(t)\le 2^{t-1}\le 2^t,
  \label{eq:degrees:partition-count}$$ since a composition is specified by cuts in the $t-1$ gaps between $t$ successive units. We set $p(0)=1$.

For a partition $\lambda\vdash s$, put $$L=\max\{\lambda_1,\lambda'_1\},\qquad h=s-L.$$ After transposing if necessary, assume its first row has length $L$. This does not change its degree. The case $h=0$ consists precisely of the row and column partitions, which are excluded from <a href="#eq:degrees:vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:vanishing">[eq:degrees:vanishing]</a>. We divide the other diagrams into three classes.

*A long first row or column: $1\le h\le s/3$.* For fixed $h$, there are at most $2p(h)$ original shapes: after a possible transpose, the diagram below the first row is a partition of $h$. By <a href="#eq:degrees:tableau-construction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:tableau-construction">[eq:degrees:tableau-construction]</a>, $$f^\lambda\ge\binom{s-h}{h}
  \ge \left(\frac{s-h}{h}\right)^h\ge 2^h.$$ Here the middle inequality follows by writing $\binom{a}{b}=\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(a-j)/(b-j)$ and bounding each factor below by $a/b$ when $a\ge b\ge1$. Thus the total inverse-degree contribution with a given $h$ is at most $$2\cdot 2^h\binom{s-h}{h}^{-16}
  \le 2\cdot 2^{-15h}.$$ For each fixed positive $h$, the binomial coefficient tends to infinity with $s$. The displayed geometric majorant is summable over $h$. Extending a contribution by zero when $h>s/3$, dominated convergence for this series shows that the total contribution of this class tends to zero.

*Intermediate width: $h>s/3$ and $L\ge s/8$.* Here $L<2s/3$, so $h>L/2$. Set $b=\lfloor L/2\rfloor$ and take the subdiagram $\mu$ consisting of the whole first row and the first $b$ boxes below it in row-major order. There are enough boxes because $h\ge b$, and an initial segment of row-major order in the lower diagram is again a Young diagram. Applying <a href="#eq:degrees:extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:extension">[eq:degrees:extension]</a> and <a href="#eq:degrees:tableau-construction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:tableau-construction">[eq:degrees:tableau-construction]</a> gives $$f^\lambda\ge \binom{L}{\lfloor L/2\rfloor}
  \ge \frac{2^L}{L+1}
  \ge \frac{2^{s/8}}{s+1}.$$ The central binomial coefficient is the largest of the $L+1$ coefficients whose sum is $2^L$. Using <a href="#eq:degrees:partition-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:partition-count">[eq:degrees:partition-count]</a>, the total contribution of this class is therefore at most <a id="eq:degrees:intermediate"></a>$$2^s\left(\frac{s+1}{2^{s/8}}\right)^{16}
  =(s+1)^{16}2^{-s}\longrightarrow0.
  \label{eq:degrees:intermediate}$$

*Small width and height: $L<s/8$.* Number row and column indices starting at $1$, and group boxes by their index sum. There are at most $2L-1$ nonempty such diagonals. Fill the diagonals in increasing index-sum order, assigning the next available consecutive labels to each diagonal in any order. Every row or column predecessor has a strictly smaller index sum, so every such filling is a standard tableau. If the nonempty diagonal sizes are $u_1,\ldots,u_q$, then $$f^\lambda\ge \prod_{j=1}^q u_j!
  \ge \prod_{j=1}^q 2^{u_j-1}
  =2^{s-q}\ge 2^{3s/4}.$$ We used $u!\ge2^{u-1}$ for integers $u\ge1$, and $q\le2L-1<s/4$. The inverse-degree contribution of this class is at most <a id="eq:degrees:small-width"></a>$$2^s\bigl(2^{3s/4}\bigr)^{-16}
  =2^{-11s}\longrightarrow0.
  \label{eq:degrees:small-width}$$

These three classes cover all partitions except the row and column, so they prove <a href="#eq:degrees:vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:vanishing">[eq:degrees:vanishing]</a>. For every $s\ge2$ those two partitions are distinct and each has degree $1$. The complete sum in <a href="#eq:degrees:uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:uniform">[eq:degrees:uniform]</a> consequently tends to $2$ as $s\to\infty$. For each fixed positive $s$ it is a finite sum of positive terms, since there are finitely many partitions and every degree is a positive integer. The tail of this convergent sequence is bounded, and its finitely many initial values have a finite maximum. This proves <a href="#eq:degrees:uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:uniform">[eq:degrees:uniform]</a>, including $s=1$, where the complete sum is $1$. ◻

</details>

In representation notation, the uniform conclusion of the lemma is <a id="eq:lift:degree-constant"></a>$$\label{eq:lift:degree-constant}
 C_0=\sup_{s\ge1}\sum_{\pi\in\widehat{S_s}}(\dim\pi)^{-16}<\infty.$$ We can now finish the transfer from bounded coset densities to Fourier decay. The bound is uniform over the block sizes because $C_0$ is uniform over all positive integers $s$.

<a id="stmt-prop-lift"></a>
<div id="prop:lift" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:lift:general-fourier"></a>
**Corollary 13** (From bounded coset densities to Fourier decay). *Let a finite set $X$ be partitioned into $k>18$ nonempty sets $M_1,\ldots,M_k$, and put $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(X)$. Let $H_a$ be the subgroup that permutes $M_a$ and fixes $X\setminus M_a$ pointwise. Suppose that a probability measure $\nu$ on $G$ satisfies, for some $B\ge1$, $$\nu(gH_a)\le B\frac{|H_a|}{|G|}
 \qquad(g\in G,\ 1\le a\le k).$$ Then every irreducible complex unitary representation $\rho$ of $G$, of dimension $D$, satisfies $$\label{eq:lift:general-fourier}
 \|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le\sqrt{kBC_0}\,D^{-(1-18/k)/2}.$$ Here $C_0$ is the absolute constant of Lemma <a href="#lem:degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degrees">12</a>. The block sizes need not be equal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-lift</summary>

*Proof.* For every $a$, the group $H_a$ is isomorphic to $S_{|M_a|}$. Hence <a href="#eq:degrees:truncated-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degrees:truncated-sum">[eq:degrees:truncated-sum]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degrees">12</a> give $$\sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widehat H_a\\\dim\pi\le D^{1/k}}}(\dim\pi)^2
 \le C_0D^{18/k}.$$ In the orbit construction of Proposition <a href="#prop:lift:orbit-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift:orbit-sum">11</a>, this recovers $\dim W_a\le C_0D^{18/k}$, and the component bound <a href="#eq:lift:component-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:component-bound">[eq:lift:component-bound]</a> becomes $$|\langle z,\widehat\nu(\rho)u_a\rangle|^2
 \le BC_0D^{-1+18/k}\|u_a\|^2\|z\|^2.$$ Substituting the displayed degree bound into <a href="#eq:lift:orbit-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:orbit-sum">[eq:lift:orbit-sum]</a> yields $$\|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
 \le kBC_0D^{-1+18/k}.$$ Taking square roots proves <a href="#eq:lift:general-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift:general-fourier">[eq:lift:general-fourier]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Completion of the mixing bound

Return to $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(V)$ for $n=2^d$. We now pass from the operator estimate to the full permutation law. Use the Fourier convention from Section <a href="#sec:lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lift">5</a>, and write $D_\rho$ for the dimension of an irreducible unitary representation $\rho$. Our convolution convention gives $\widehat{\mu*\nu}(\rho)=\widehat\mu(\rho)\widehat\nu(\rho)$. The following Plancherel bound is the classical Fourier method used by Diaconis and Shahshahani <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[DiaconisShahshahani1981](#ref-DiaconisShahshahani1981)]. We keep the full matrix form because the shuffle law need not be central, and we record the normalization explicitly.

<a id="stmt-lem-plancherel"></a>
<div id="lem:plancherel" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:plancherel-tv"></a>
**Lemma 14** (The full-group variation bound). *For any probability measure $\nu$ on $G$, $$\label{eq:plancherel-tv}
 4\|\nu-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^2
 \le\sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv}}D_\rho
       \|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2,$$ where the sum contains one representative of each nontrivial irreducible equivalence class.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plancherel"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plancherel</summary>

*Proof.* Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the left side by $|G|\sum_g|\nu(g)-|G|^{-1}|^2$. For any real function $a$ on $G$, expansion and the regular-character identity give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_\rho D_\rho\Bigl\|\sum_g a(g)\rho(g)\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 &=\sum_{g,h}a(g)a(h)
   \sum_\rho D_\rho\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho(h)^*\rho(g)\bigr)\\
 &=|G|\sum_g a(g)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Apply this to $a(g)=\nu(g)-|G|^{-1}$. The trivial coefficient vanishes; uniform averaging in every nontrivial irreducible is zero. These facts give <a href="#eq:plancherel-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:plancherel-tv">[eq:plancherel-tv]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-fair-sign"></a>
<div id="lem:fair-sign" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Fair sign). *For every $d\ge1$ and every integer $t\ge1$, $\widehat{q_d^{*t}}(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})=0$. If a probability measure $\nu$ is $\varepsilon$ away from $q_d^{*t}$ in total variation, then $|\widehat\nu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|\le2\varepsilon$. If instead $0\le\xi\le q_d^{*t}$ has mass $m$, then $|\widehat\xi(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|\le1-m$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fair-sign"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fair-sign</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on every switch bit except one in the final physical shuffle. Changing that bit composes the output permutation with a transposition, so its two possible signs are opposite and equally likely. The expectation of sign is therefore zero. The two perturbation estimates follow by summing against a function of absolute value one; their respective $\ell^1$ differences are $2\varepsilon$ and $1-m$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fair-sign-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fair-sign</summary>

<a id="eq:final-trim"></a>
<a id="eq:final-op"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $k=64$ and let $d\ge6$ grow. Put $T=1025d$ and $\mu=q_d^{*T}$. Proposition <a href="#prop:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marginals">9</a> supplies a partition of the labels into $k$ sets of size $m=n/k$ for which the sum of the complementary marginal errors tends to zero. Lemma <a href="#lem:trim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trim">10</a> therefore applies with their sum at most $1/4$ for all sufficiently large $d$. It supplies a probability measure $\bar\mu$ such that $$\label{eq:final-trim}
 \|\bar\mu-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1),\qquad
 \bar\mu(gH_a)\le4\frac{|H_a|}{|G|}\quad(g\in G,\ 1\le a\le k).$$ Here $H_a$ permutes the $a$th label block and fixes all other labels. Corollary <a href="#prop:lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift">13</a> gives, simultaneously for every irreducible $\rho$ of dimension $D$, $$\label{eq:final-op}
 \|\widehat{\bar\mu}(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le A D^{-23/64},\qquad A=\sqrt{4\cdot64\,C_0},$$ where $C_0$ is the absolute constant in Lemma <a href="#lem:degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degrees">12</a>.

<a id="eq:final-degree-sum"></a>
Take $\ell=32$, a fixed number independent of $d$. Operator-norm submultiplicativity and $\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\sqrt D\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ show that the contribution of the nontrivial, nonsign representations to the right side of <a href="#eq:plancherel-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:plancherel-tv">[eq:plancherel-tv]</a>, for $\nu=\bar\mu^{*\ell}$, is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv},\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}}
       D\|\widehat{\bar\mu}(\rho)^\ell\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 &\le A^{64}\sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv},\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}}
       D^{2-64(23/64)}\notag\\
 &=A^{64}\sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv},\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}}D^{-21}=o(1).
 \label{eq:final-degree-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ The last conclusion follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degrees">12</a>, since $D^{-21}\le D^{-16}$ for $D\ge1$. Neither diagonalizability nor normality of the Fourier matrices is needed here.

The sign representation has dimension one, so <a href="#eq:final-op" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-op">[eq:final-op]</a> alone gives no decay. Lemma <a href="#lem:fair-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fair-sign">15</a> shows that $\widehat\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})=0$ and therefore $$|\widehat{\bar\mu}(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|
 \le2\|\bar\mu-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$ Its contribution after $\ell$ factors is therefore $o(1)$ as well. Together with Lemma <a href="#lem:plancherel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plancherel">14</a> and <a href="#eq:final-degree-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-degree-sum">[eq:final-degree-sum]</a>, this proves $\|\bar\mu^{*32}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1)$.

Convolution by a probability measure contracts total variation. Replacing the factors one at a time and using <a href="#eq:final-trim" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-trim">[eq:final-trim]</a> gives $$\|\mu^{*32}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le32\|\mu-\bar\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
       +\|\bar\mu^{*32}-U\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$ Successive independent $T$-step blocks of the original shuffle have law $\mu$, so $\mu^{*32}=q_d^{*32800d}$. Equation <a href="#eq:worst-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:worst-state">[eq:worst-state]</a> makes this conclusion uniform over the initial ordering. In particular the distance is at most $1/4$ for all sufficiently large $d$, as required. ◻

</details>

The constants were chosen to leave room in two simple estimates: the hidden-card contraction must overcome the number of sequential marginal comparisons, and the fixed convolution count must overcome the Fourier dimension weights. No optimal leading constant or cutoff statement is asserted. Together with the support lower bound, the result gives mixing order $\Theta(d)$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### Further partial-permutation laws.

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is complete with the frame, omitted-block, orbit, and tableau arguments above. The remaining sections compare estimates for each fixed list with estimates averaged over lists. They then examine what can be retained after stopping or conditioning on trajectory events and develop Fourier and character transfers for those forms of partial information.

<a id="shear:sec:directions"></a>
### Fixed input laws and independent shears

The local proof obtained balance by sampling one omitted block.
