For an arbitrary fixed input list, a previous switch supplies balance instead: it crosses the two halves relevant to the next direction, and the intervening switches preserve those halves. This gives an ordinary tuple law for every fixed list. We then record an independent shear realization of fresh directions and several estimates that retain an average over inputs. The realization will also control which auxiliary data may be disclosed in the later history arguments.

<a id="shear:eq:coordinate-chain"></a>
We use $N=n=2^d$ and the coordinate process $X^{\boldsymbol\iota}$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a>, where $\boldsymbol\iota=(\iota_1,\ldots,\iota_d)$ is any ordering of the standard axes and $i(s)=\iota_{1+((s-1)\bmod d)}$. Thus $$\label{shear:eq:coordinate-chain}
 X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_0=\mathrm{id},\qquad
 X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_s=h_sX^{\boldsymbol\iota}_{s-1},\qquad
 h_s\sim U_{C_{e_{i(s)}}}$$ with independent increments. The order $(1,\ldots,d)$ gives the rotating-frame chain $X$; its physical permutation is $R^sX_s$. Other starting phases and the reversed order are included by choosing another $\boldsymbol\iota$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Balance for every fixed list

We will use the following elementary concentration estimates.

<a id="stmt-lem-concentration"></a>
<div id="lem:concentration" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Concentration estimates). *Let $X_1,\ldots,X_r$ be independent random variables in $[0,1]$ and put $S=\sum_iX_i$. For $a\ge0$ and $r\ge1$, $$\Pr(S-\mathbb ES\ge a),\quad\Pr(S-\mathbb ES\le-a)\le e^{-2a^2/r}.$$ More generally, if $(M_j)_{j=0}^r$ is a real martingale whose increments have absolute value at most $c_j$, either tail at distance $a$ is at most $\exp[-a^2/(2\sum_jc_j^2)]$ when the denominator is positive. If all $c_j$ vanish, the martingale is constant. In particular, if $X$ counts the points of a uniform $m$-subset in a fixed half of a finite set of even size, then $$\Pr(X<m/4)\le e^{-m/32}\qquad(m\ge1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* For a variable $Z$ in an interval of length $b$, put $\psi(s)=\log\mathbb Ee^{sZ}$. Its second derivative is the variance under the exponentially tilted law, at most $b^2/4$. Integrating from $\psi(0)=0$ and $\psi'(0)=\mathbb EZ$ gives $\mathbb Ee^{s(Z-\mathbb EZ)}\le e^{s^2b^2/8}$. Multiplication and minimization in $s$ prove the independent-variable bound. The conditional version, with interval length $2c_j$, gives $\mathbb Ee^{s(M_r-M_0)}\le e^{s^2\sum_jc_j^2/2}$ and the martingale bound. These are the exponential-moment arguments underlying the inequalities of Hoeffding and Azuma <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[hoeffding1963](#ref-hoeffding1963), [azuma1967](#ref-azuma1967)].

Expose a uniform subset in a uniform random order and form the Doob martingale for its final half-count. The expected final count changes by at most one at each draw: completions following two possible next draws can be coupled by exchanging those points. The initial expectation is $m/2$. Use $r=m$, $c_j=1$, and $a=m/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="shear:eq:hyperplane"></a>
For the fixed-list estimate, generate $Z$ directly as in Section <a href="#sec:marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:marginals">4</a>: its first $d$ directions are $v_j=e_{\iota_j}$, and for $s>d$ choose $v_s$ uniformly outside the hyperplane $$\label{shear:eq:hyperplane}
 W_s=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(v_{s-d+1},\ldots,v_{s-1}).$$ Given the complete schedule, the switches are independent and uniform. Every $d$ consecutive directions form a basis. For a fixed input list, take deterministic initial data $\Xi$ in <a href="#eq:marginal-filtration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-filtration">[eq:marginal-filtration]</a>.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-fixed-balance"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:fixed-balance" class="lemma">

<a id="shear:eq:fixed-balance"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Balance after the previous transverse switch). *Fix any $\ell<N$ observed labels, and put $m=N-\ell$. For $s>d$, let $D_{s-1}$ be their available positions just before step $s$. Then $$\label{shear:eq:fixed-balance}
 \Pr\{\min(|D_{s-1}\cap W_s|,|D_{s-1}\setminus W_s|)<m/4\}
 \le2e^{-m/8}.$$ The probability averages the observed paths; it is not conditional on them.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-fixed-balance"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-fixed-balance</summary>

*Proof.* Fix the complete direction schedule. The direction $v_{s-d}$ crosses the two cosets of $W_s$, because the $d$ directions starting at $s-d$ form a basis. The intervening directions lie in $W_s$ and preserve each coset.

Condition on the observed configuration immediately before step $s-d$. Let $a$ be the number of matching pairs with two available positions and $b$ the number with one, so $m=2a+b$. Immediately after this crossing switch, the available count on either chosen side is $a+\operatorname{Bin}(b,1/2)$, using $b$ independent fair coins. Its mean is $m/2$. If $b>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:concentration">16</a> bounds a deviation of at least $m/4$ by $2\exp(-2(m/4)^2/b)\le2e^{-m/8}$; if $b=0$ the count is exactly $m/2$. The intervening switches preserve the count. Remove the conditioning to obtain the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shear-prop-fixed-marginal"></a>
<div id="shear:prop:fixed-marginal" class="proposition">

<a id="shear:eq:general-fixed-marginal"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Uniform large-marginal estimates). *Let $L\ge2$ be a fixed integer dividing $N$, and let $0\le q\le N-N/L$. For every ordered list $C$ of $q$ distinct labels, every periodic ordering $\boldsymbol\iota$ of the standard axes, and every integer $T\ge d$, $$\label{shear:eq:general-fixed-marginal}
 \|\mathcal L(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T(C))-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac{N^{3/2}}2
 \left(e^{-(T-d)/(4L)}+2e^{-N/(8L)}\right)^{1/2}.$$ The same bound holds for $(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T)^{-1}(C)$. For the physical shuffle and its inverse, the corresponding position laws satisfy the same bound.*

<a id="shear:eq:256-marginal"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:257-marginal"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:2049-marginal"></a>
*The following choices used below give distance at most $N^{3/2}\sqrt{a_T}/2$: $$\begin{aligned}
 L=8,\quad T=256d:&\quad
 a_T=(31/32)^{255d}+64e^{-N/64},
 \label{shear:eq:256-marginal}\\
 L=8,\quad T=257d:&\quad
 a_T=e^{-8d}+2e^{-N/64},
 \label{shear:eq:257-marginal}\\
 L=32,\quad T=2049d:&\quad
 a_T=e^{-16d}+256e^{-N/256}.
 \label{shear:eq:2049-marginal}
\end{aligned}$$ Each of these three substituted bounds tends to zero as $d\to\infty$, uniformly in the list and axis order. The last energy bound is $o(N^{-4})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-prop-fixed-marginal"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-prop-fixed-marginal</summary>

<a id="shear:eq:fixed-recursion"></a>
*Proof.* For a successive hidden-card problem, its number $m$ of available positions is at least $N/L$. Off the event in <a href="#shear:eq:fixed-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:fixed-balance">[shear:eq:fixed-balance]</a>, Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:energy">7</a> gives conditional expected loss at least $\gamma\Delta_t$, where $\gamma=1/(4L)$. The loss is always nonnegative. Equation <a href="#eq:marginal-balance-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-balance-recurrence">[eq:marginal-balance-recurrence]</a> therefore gives, for $t\ge d$, $$\label{shear:eq:fixed-recursion}
 \mathbb E\Delta_{t+1}\le(1-\gamma)\mathbb E\Delta_t
       +2\gamma e^{-N/(8L)}.$$ Starting from $\Delta_d\le1$ yields $\mathbb E\Delta_T\le(1-\gamma)^{T-d}+2e^{-N/(8L)}$. Apply Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a> with terminal data $\Lambda=\mathbf v$. For every complete schedule, the first basis is $(e_{\iota_1},\ldots,e_{\iota_d})$, so $L_0=I$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a>. The same-list equality <a href="#eq:frames:tuple-input-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:tuple-input-map">[eq:frames:tuple-input-map]</a> transfers the conditional tuple bound to $X^{\boldsymbol\iota}$, giving <a href="#shear:eq:general-fixed-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:general-fixed-marginal">[shear:eq:general-fixed-marginal]</a>.

Each switch is an involution. Reversing the independent factors therefore identifies $(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T)^{-1}$ with a coordinate chain whose first axis is $i(T)$ and whose periodic order is reversed. This is another permitted ordering, so the same estimate applies. The relation $Y_T=R^TX_T$ transfers the forward physical law by an output bijection. Its inverse is $X_T^{-1}R^{-T}$; the input bijection only changes the fixed list, and the bound is uniform in that list.

For <a href="#shear:eq:256-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:256-marginal">[shear:eq:256-marginal]</a>, weaken the additive term in <a href="#shear:eq:fixed-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:fixed-recursion">[shear:eq:fixed-recursion]</a> to $2e^{-N/64}$ and sum the geometric series, giving the factor $64$. At $257d$ keep the sharper recurrence and use $(31/32)^{256d}\le e^{-8d}$. More generally, at $T=(1+64L)d$, the weakened additive term $2e^{-N/(8L)}$ gives $e^{-16d}+8Le^{-N/(8L)}$, yielding <a href="#shear:eq:2049-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:2049-marginal">[shear:eq:2049-marginal]</a>. These rates dominate the factor $N^3$ in the squared tuple bound, and $e^{-16d}=o(N^{-4})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### An independent shear realization

The fixed-list proof above uses the directly generated direction process. The next construction couples a process with a triangular first basis and the same later uniform-complement rule to a coordinate chain with the same fixed inputs. It also proves the conditional path identity needed when the auxiliary shear entries are retained at terminal time.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-keys"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:keys" class="lemma">

<a id="shear:eq:prefix-factorization"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Independent shear realization). *Fix an axis order $\boldsymbol\iota$ and a horizon $T\ge d$. Independently of the increments in <a href="#shear:eq:coordinate-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:coordinate-chain">[shear:eq:coordinate-chain]</a>, choose independent fair bits $b_{sj}$ for $j\ne i(s)$, put $b_{s,i(s)}=0$, and write $\Lambda$ for all these entries. Set $$S_s=I+e_{i(s)}b_s,\qquad
 A_0=I,\quad A_s=A_{s-1}S_s,\qquad
 v_s=A_{s-1}e_{i(s)},\qquad
 Z_s=A_sX^{\boldsymbol\iota}_s.$$ Conditional on $\Lambda$, the increments of $Z$ are independent uniform switches in directions $v_s$. More precisely, if $\mathsf P_{v_1,\ldots,v_t}$ is the product-switch path law through time $t$ starting at the identity, then $$\label{shear:eq:prefix-factorization}
 \mathcal L((Z_s)_{s=0}^t\mid\Lambda)
 =\mathsf P_{v_1,\ldots,v_t}\qquad(0\le t\le T).$$ Every $d$ consecutive directions form a basis. For $s>d$, conditional on $v_1,\ldots,v_{s-1}$, the next direction is uniform on $V\setminus W_s$, with $W_s$ as in <a href="#shear:eq:hyperplane" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:hyperplane">[shear:eq:hyperplane]</a>. This law is unchanged by additionally observing any part of the virtual path prefix through time $s-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-keys"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-keys</summary>

*Proof.* The map $S_s$ is an involution in $C_{e_{i(s)}}$, because $b_se_{i(s)}=0$. The increment of $Z$ is $$A_{s-1}(S_sh_s)A_{s-1}^{-1}.$$ For fixed $\Lambda$, the factors $S_sh_s$ are independent and uniform in their coordinate matching groups. Conjugation gives the asserted product-switch law, including the joint prefix identity <a href="#shear:eq:prefix-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:prefix-factorization">[shear:eq:prefix-factorization]</a>.

Extend $i(s)$ periodically beyond $T$ and define the deterministic next-read time of a column by $$\tau(s,j)=\min\{t>s:i(t)=j\}\qquad(j\ne i(s)).$$ Right multiplication by $S_s$ leaves the selected column unchanged and adds $b_{sj}v_s$ to column $j$. Thus the direction at a column’s first read, for $t\le d$, is $$v_t=e_{i(t)}+\sum_{1\le u<t}b_{u,i(t)}v_u.$$ These first $d$ directions form a triangular basis. At later reads, <a id="shear:eq:key-recurrence"></a>$$\label{shear:eq:key-recurrence}
 v_t=v_{t-d}+\sum_{t-d<u<t}b_{u,i(t)}v_u\qquad(t>d).$$ In either display the coefficients used at time $t$ are exactly the entries whose next-read time is $t$. Inductively, all earlier directions are functions only of entries with next-read time less than $t$. The batch read at $t$ is therefore independent and fair conditional on those directions. The recurrence also shows inductively that every consecutive $d$ directions form a basis. For $t>d$, its fresh $d-1$ coefficients make $v_t$ uniform on $v_{t-d}+W_t=V\setminus W_t$.

Finally, <a href="#shear:eq:prefix-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:prefix-factorization">[shear:eq:prefix-factorization]</a> says that the entire virtual path prefix and $\Lambda$ are conditionally independent given the direction prefix. Hence observing any portion of that path prefix cannot reveal the fresh batch that determines the next direction. ◻

</details>

<a id="shear:eq:terminal-factorization"></a>
For initial data $\Xi$ independent of $(\Lambda,(Z_s)_{s=0}^T)$ and determining the observed list, the joint prefix factorization gives, for every $0\le t\le T$, $1\le j\le q$, and $x\in V$, $$\label{shear:eq:terminal-factorization}
 \Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x\mid\Lambda,\Xi,\,
              Z_s(c_i):0\le s\le t,\ i<j\}
 =p_t^j(x),$$ where $p_t^j$ uses the chronological field <a href="#eq:marginal-filtration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-filtration">[eq:marginal-filtration]</a>. At $t=T$ this is precisely the terminal identification required by Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a>. For $t<T$ it concerns only predecessor path prefixes through $t$; conditioning on their future paths requires the separate path-cylinder argument in Section <a href="#frames:chapter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frames:chapter">12</a>. The next direction is averaged in the smaller chronological field before $\Lambda$ is disclosed.

The coupling also preserves every fixed input list conditionally on the shears: <a id="shear:eq:fixed-list-frame"></a>$$\label{shear:eq:fixed-list-frame}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\Lambda)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =\|\mathcal L(X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T(C))-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ Indeed, $X^{\boldsymbol\iota}$ is independent of $\Lambda$ and $Z_T=A_TX^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T$, with $A_T$ a bijection on outputs. Here $A_0=I$ although the first directions are generally triangular combinations of the coordinate axes. This same-input identity is a feature of the shear realization; the arbitrary first-basis statement in Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a> instead has the input map $L_0^{-1}$.

<a id="stmt-shear-rem-factorization"></a>
<div id="shear:rem:factorization" class="remark">

<a id="shear:eq:inverse-coupling"></a>
*Remark 20* (Equivalent inverse-frame factorization). The same coupling has a useful inverse notation. Let $Q_s$ be the original independent coordinate increments and choose the independent shears $S_s$ above. Put $B_0=I$ and $B_s=S_sB_{s-1}=A_s^{-1}$. Define $$\label{shear:eq:inverse-coupling}
 M_s=B_{s-1}^{-1}(S_sQ_s)B_{s-1}
     =B_s^{-1}Q_sB_{s-1},\qquad
 X^{\boldsymbol\iota}_T=B_TM_T\cdots M_1.$$ The second identity follows by telescoping. Conditional on all $S_s$, the factors $S_sQ_s$ are independent uniform coordinate switches, so $M_s$ has virtual direction $B_{s-1}^{-1}e_{i(s)}$. This is the same virtual process and the same prefix and terminal factorization as above. The map $B_T$ acts only on outputs, leaving a fixed input list unchanged. The posterior identity concerns virtual positions; once $B_T$ is disclosed, both the virtual endpoint law and its uniform comparison may be pushed through that known output map.

</div>

The fixed-list balance has a second interpretation in this coupling. For $s>d$, the current columns of $A_{s-1}$ other than column $i(s)$ are triangular combinations of the last $d-1$ selected columns, with diagonal coefficients one. Hence $$W_s=A_{s-1}\{x:x_{i(s)}=0\}.$$ The two available counts in <a href="#shear:eq:fixed-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:fixed-balance">[shear:eq:fixed-balance]</a> are therefore the counts according to bit $i(s)$ in the coordinate chain. That bit was last refreshed at step $s-d$ and is unchanged by the intervening coordinate layers. The same $a+\operatorname{Bin}(b,1/2)$ calculation proves the balance directly in the original coordinate frame.

<a id="shear:sec:averaged"></a>
### Estimates that average the input labels

Sampling the inputs supplies balance without using the previous transverse switch. These estimates average the distance for each specified input list; they do not replace that family of conditional distances by the distance of a mixture. This distinction permits a later choice of one deterministic partition or the construction of kernels for all starting states.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A reservoir of omitted labels

<a id="stmt-shear-prop-reservoir"></a>
<div id="shear:prop:reservoir" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (A reservoir of omitted labels). *Let $d\ge3$, $b=N/8$, and $T=257d$. Let $B$ be a uniform $b$-subset of input labels, independent of the shuffle. For any fixed ordering of $V\setminus B$, let $\delta_T(B)$ be the distance of its image tuple under $X_T$ from uniform distinct positions. With $\theta=2^{1/4}(3/4)<1$, <a id="shear:eq:reservoir-marginal"></a>$$\label{shear:eq:reservoir-marginal}
 \mathbb E_B\delta_T(B)
 \le\frac{N^{3/2}}2
 \left(e^{-8d}+2(N+1)\theta^{N/8}\right)^{1/2}=o(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-prop-reservoir"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-prop-reservoir</summary>

*Proof.* Use the direct direction process with standard first basis, sampled independently of $B$, and take $\Xi=B$ in the online construction. At every time the available set for a hidden card contains $Z_t(B)$. Fix the full direction schedule and the full permutation $Z_t$, but not $B$. The set $Z_t(B)$ is then a uniform $b$-subset, and each coset of the next-direction hyperplane has size $N/2$.

Its count in a specified half has the distribution of the number of ones among the first $b$ of $N$ independent fair bits, conditional on their total being $N/2$. This conditioning event has probability at least $1/(N+1)$, because the central binomial coefficient is maximal. For the unconditioned first-$b$ count $J$, $$\Pr\{J\le b/4\}
 \le2^{b/4}\mathbb E2^{-J}
 =\bigl(2^{1/4}3/4\bigr)^b.$$ The inclusion of $Z_t(B)$ in the available set and a union bound give $p_{\mathrm{bad}}=2(N+1)\theta^b$ as an upper bound for the probability that either available half has fewer than $b/4$ positions. This bound is unconditional in the observed paths.

Off this event the conditional expected loss is at least $\Delta_t/32$. Equations <a href="#eq:marginal-balance-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-balance-recurrence">[eq:marginal-balance-recurrence]</a> and <a href="#eq:marginal-cross-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-cross-energy">[eq:marginal-cross-energy]</a> give $$\mathbb E\Delta_{t+1}\le(31/32)\mathbb E\Delta_t+p_{\mathrm{bad}}/32,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\Delta_T\le e^{-8d}+p_{\mathrm{bad}}.$$ Use Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a> with $\Lambda=\mathbf v$. For each fixed schedule and $B$, the standard first basis gives $L_0=I$, so the conditional auxiliary distance equals the distance for $X_T$ on that same complement. Averaging over $B$ proves the bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Uniform input tuples after a deterministic first sweep

<a id="stmt-lifts-averaged-tuples"></a>
<div id="lifts:averaged-tuples" class="lemma">

<a id="lifts:averaged-bound"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Averaged tuple bound). *Fix an integer $b\ge2$ dividing $N$, put $m=N/b$, and let $t\ge d$ be an integer. Let $C$ be a uniformly sampled ordered tuple of $N-m$ distinct input labels, independent of the shuffle, and let $\mu_C$ be its time-$t$ physical image law with $C$ fixed. Then $$\label{lifts:averaged-bound}
 \mathbb E_C\|\mu_C-\pi_{N-m}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac{N^{3/2}}2
 \left((1-1/(4b))^{t-d}+2\vartheta^{N/b}\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad \vartheta=\frac32\,2^{-3/4}<1.$$ The same bound holds for a fixed ordered list of $N-m$ labels when its conditional distance is averaged over a uniformly chosen starting deck.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lifts-averaged-tuples"><summary>Proof of stmt-lifts-averaged-tuples</summary>

*Proof.* Use the direct process with the standard first basis and take $\Xi=C$. For a hidden label at index $j$, let $h=N-j+1\ge m$ be the number of available positions. Fix the complete direction and switch history, but not $C$. At each fixed time, the preceding positions form a uniform ordered sample, so the available set is a uniform $h$-subset. The next-direction hyperplane is fixed under this conditioning.

<a id="shear:eq:uniform-subset-moment"></a>
If $X$ is the count of a uniform $h$-subset in a fixed half, expand the product of $1+I_i$ over an ordered sample without replacement. Any specified $\ell$ sample indices lie in that half with probability at most $2^{-\ell}$. Thus $$\label{shear:eq:uniform-subset-moment}
 \mathbb E2^X\le(3/2)^h,\qquad
 \Pr\{\min(X,h-X)<h/4\}\le2\vartheta^h.$$ The second inequality follows by applying Markov’s inequality to the count in each half above $3h/4$. It is used only after averaging out the input list, not conditional on observed paths.

On the balanced event, the conditional expected loss is at least $h\Delta_s/(4N)$. With $\gamma_j=h/(4N)$, the unconditional recurrence and its iteration give $$\mathbb E\Delta_{s+1}\le(1-\gamma_j)\mathbb E\Delta_s
                   +2\gamma_j\vartheta^h,\qquad
 \mathbb E\Delta_t\le(1-1/(4b))^{t-d}+2\vartheta^m.$$ Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a>, with $\Lambda=\mathbf v$, bounds the average distance conditional on the full schedule and list. For every fixed schedule the first basis is standard, so the same-list frame equality transfers that distance to $X_t$; the terminal rotation transfers it to the physical shuffle. This proves <a href="#lifts:averaged-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lifts:averaged-bound">[lifts:averaged-bound]</a>. A uniform starting deck sends any fixed ordered label list to a uniform input tuple, which proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lifts-tuple-constants"></a>
<div id="lifts:tuple-constants" class="corollary">

**Corollary 23** (Block lengths for the averaged input). *For $b=16$, the average distance in Lemma <a href="#lifts:averaged-tuples" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lifts:averaged-tuples">22</a> at $t=513d$ is at most $$\varepsilon_N=\frac12N^{3/2}
       \left(N^{-8}+2\vartheta^{N/16}\right)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ At $t=2049d$ it is at most $N^{-10}$ for sufficiently large $d$. For $b=1024$ and $d\ge10$, time $t=(1+32b)d$ gives average distance $o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lifts-tuple-constants"><summary>Proof of stmt-lifts-tuple-constants</summary>

*Proof.* The respective contracting times are $512d$, $2048d$, and $32bd$, with energy factors at most $e^{-8d}$, $e^{-32d}$, and $e^{-8d}$. Use $e^{-8d}\le N^{-8}$ for the first and $N^{3/2}e^{-16d}=o(N^{-10})$ for the second. Exponential decay in $N$ handles the other term in each case. ◻

</details>

There is also a concentration proof of the last assertion that uses the subset martingale in Lemma <a href="#lem:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:concentration">16</a>. A union bound over the two halves gives imbalance probability at most $2e^{-h/32}$. With $c_0=1/32$, the weaker recurrence <a id="lifts:azuma-recurrence"></a>$$\label{lifts:azuma-recurrence}
 \mathbb E\Delta_{s+1}\le(1-1/(4b))\mathbb E\Delta_s+2e^{-c_0N/b}$$ gives $\mathbb E\Delta_t\le e^{-8d}+8b e^{-c_0N/b}$ at $t=(1+32b)d$. The expected endpoint error of one conditional card law is $o(1/N)$ and its sum is $o(1)$. This is an alternative proof of the averaged input used later for omitted sets.

<a id="frames:average-section"></a>
#### Sampling inputs with a random initial basis

To begin contraction before a deterministic first sweep, choose $v_{t+1}$ uniformly outside the span $W_t$ of the last $\min(t,d-1)$ directions, with $W_0=\{0\}$. Use independent fair switches conditional on the complete schedule. Each successive set of at most $d$ directions is independent, so Lemma <a href="#lem:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frames">[lem:frames]</a> applies when $T\ge d$. Choose a hyperplane $J_t\supseteq W_t$ by a fixed rule based on the direction prefix.

<a id="shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer"></a>
Let $C$ be a uniform ordered list, independent of the joint schedule and process, and take $\Xi=C$. The online energy calculation uses only the chronological field <a href="#eq:marginal-filtration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marginal-filtration">[eq:marginal-filtration]</a>. At terminal time Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:tuple">8</a> first bounds $$\mathbb E_{\mathbf v,C}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\mathbf v,C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ For each fixed schedule, the general frame comparison then gives $$\label{shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer}
 \mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\mathbf v,C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =
 \mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(X_T(C)\mid C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.$$ Here the reference coordinate order is $(1,\ldots,d)$. The map $L_0^{-1}$ disappears only after averaging $C$. It is determined once the first $d$ directions have been revealed; we use it only in the terminal comparison and do not adjoin it before those defining directions are available.

<a id="stmt-shear-prop-random-tuple"></a>
<div id="shear:prop:random-tuple" class="proposition">

<a id="shear:eq:random-tuple"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Averaged tuples with no initial deterministic sweep). *Let $d\ge3$, $q=7N/8$, and $T=1024d$. For a uniformly sampled ordered distinct input tuple $C$, independent of $X$, $$\label{shear:eq:random-tuple}
 \mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(X_T(C)\mid C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le\frac{q\sqrt N}{2}
 \left(e^{-16d}+2(N+1)e^{-N/256}\right)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ The corresponding hidden-card energy is $O(N^{-20})$, uniformly over its index.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-prop-random-tuple"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-prop-random-tuple</summary>

*Proof.* For the hidden label at index $j$, let $h=N-j+1\ge N/8$. Conditional on the full direction and switch history but not on $C$, its available set is a uniform $h$-subset. For its count in the fixed half $J_t$, choose each point independently with probability $h/N$ and condition on selecting exactly $h$ points. That size has probability at least $1/(N+1)$: it is a mode of the binomial size distribution, as comparison of consecutive probabilities shows. Before conditioning, the half-count has mean $h/2$ and is a sum of $N/2$ independent bounded variables. Lemma <a href="#lem:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:concentration">16</a> gives <a id="shear:eq:sample-balance"></a>$$\label{shear:eq:sample-balance}
 \Pr\{\min(|D_t^j\cap J_t|,|D_t^j\setminus J_t|)<h/4\}
 \le2(N+1)e^{-h^2/(4N)}
 \le\beta_N,\qquad
 \beta_N=2(N+1)e^{-N/256}.$$ For $h=N$ the count is deterministic and the bound is automatic.

The proper-subspace part of Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:energy">7</a> gives conditional expected loss at least $h\Delta_t/(8N)\ge\Delta_t/64$ off this event. Hence, from time zero, $$\mathbb E\Delta_{t+1}\le(63/64)\mathbb E\Delta_t+\beta_N/64,\qquad
 \mathbb E\Delta_T\le e^{-T/64}+\beta_N
             =e^{-16d}+\beta_N=O(N^{-20}).$$ Apply the terminal tuple lemma with $\Lambda=\mathbf v$ and then the per-schedule averaged equality <a href="#shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer">[shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer]</a>. This proves <a href="#shear:eq:random-tuple" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:random-tuple">[shear:eq:random-tuple]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-frames-average-law"></a>
<div id="frames:average-law" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Ordinary random-input marginals). *Let $d\ge6$, $b=64$, $q=N(1-1/b)$, and $T=2048d$. The average, over uniform ordered lists of $q$ distinct input labels, of their time-$T$ physical image-law distance from $\pi_q$ is $o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frames-average-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-frames-average-law</summary>

*Proof.* Use the random initial basis process above. At a hidden index, the number $h$ of available positions is at least $N/b$. Conditional on the complete direction and switch history, and before sampling the list, this set is a uniform $h$-subset. Thus <a href="#shear:eq:uniform-subset-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:uniform-subset-moment">[shear:eq:uniform-subset-moment]</a> bounds its imbalance across the chosen $J_t$ by $2\vartheta^h$.

<a id="frames:average-tv"></a>
On the balanced event the proper-subspace contraction gives loss at least $\Delta_t/(8b)$. Using the weaker additive term $2\vartheta^{N/b}$ in the unconditional recurrence yields $$\mathbb E\Delta_T\le(1-1/(8b))^T+2T\vartheta^{N/b}.$$ The terminal tuple comparison and then <a href="#shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer">[shear:eq:sampled-frame-transfer]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf v,C}
 \|\mathcal L(Z_T(C)\mid\mathbf v,C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 &=\mathbb E_C\|\mathcal L(X_T(C)\mid C)-\pi_q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\notag\\
 &\le\frac12N^{3/2}
 \left((1-1/(8b))^T+2T\vartheta^{N/b}\right)^{1/2}.
 \label{frames:average-tv}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $(1-1/(8b))^T\le e^{-4d}$, so the right side tends to zero. Since $T$ is a multiple of $d$, $X_T=Y_T$ in the rotating-frame coupling, which proves the physical statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="shear:sec:delayed-symmetry"></a>
### Symmetry accumulated between two visits to a coordinate

Intervening coordinate switches produce a symmetry that can be used at an earlier conditioning time. Between two visits to a coordinate, they make the available set and its conditional mass vector invariant in law under every translation of one coordinate half. This yields an energy-loss bound conditional on the earlier visit: the two half counts are already fixed at that time, while the intervening switches supply the symmetry.

<a id="stmt-shear-lem-delayed-symmetry"></a>
<div id="shear:lem:delayed-symmetry" class="lemma">

<a id="shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-loss"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Delayed half-space symmetry). *Let $V=\mathbb F_2^d$, $N=2^d$, and fix a coordinate $i$. Let $j_1,\ldots,j_{d-1}$ list all coordinates other than $i$, in any order. Condition on a sigma field $\mathcal A$ which specifies a nonempty set $B_0\subseteq V$ of size $h$ and a probability vector $p_0$ supported on $B_0$. Independently of $\mathcal A$, let $S_r$ be independent uniform matching switches in direction $e_{j_r}$, for $1\le r\le d-1$. Define $B_r=S_rB_{r-1}$. On each matching edge, obtain $p_r$ by averaging the two masses when both endpoints belong to $B_{r-1}$, and by moving the mass with its unique available endpoint when exactly one endpoint belongs to $B_{r-1}$; it is zero on edges with no available endpoint. Put $$a_r(x)=p_r(x)-h^{-1}\mathbf1_{B_r}(x),\qquad
 E_r=\sum_{x\in V}a_r(x)^2,
 \qquad H_b=\{x\in V:x_i=b\}\quad(b=0,1).$$ For each $z\in V$ with $z_i=0$, let $T_zx=x+x_i z$. Conditional on $\mathcal A$, the terminal pair $(B_{d-1},a_{d-1})$ has the same law as <a id="shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-invariance"></a>$$\bigl(T_zB_{d-1},\ a_{d-1}\circ T_z^{-1}\bigr).
 \label{shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-invariance}$$ The counts $h_b=|B_r\cap H_b|$ are independent of $r$ and are $\mathcal A$-measurable. If a further independent fair matching switch in direction $e_i$ produces the energy $E_d$ by the same rule, then $$\mathbb E[E_{d-1}-E_d\mid\mathcal A]
 \ge\frac{\min(h_0,h_1)}N\,
             \mathbb E[E_{d-1}\mid\mathcal A].
 \label{shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-loss}$$ The statement includes $d=1$, when the intermediate schedule is empty.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-lem-delayed-symmetry"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-lem-delayed-symmetry</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $z$ with $z_i=0$, and set $$z_0=0,\qquad z_r=\sum_{s=1}^r z_{j_s}e_{j_s}
       \quad(1\le r\le d-1).$$ Thus $z_{d-1}=z$. Replace the $r$th switch by $$S'_r=T_{z_r}S_rT_{z_{r-1}}^{-1}.$$ We verify that the replaced switches have exactly the original joint law. For $j\ne i$, $T_w e_j=e_j$, so conjugation by $T_w$ maps the matching subgroup in direction $e_j$ onto itself. Also $$T_{z_r}T_{z_{r-1}}^{-1}(x)
   =x+x_i z_{j_r}e_{j_r}$$ is a fixed member of that matching subgroup: it either swaps or fixes each pair, since $x_i$ is constant on a coordinate-$j_r$ pair. Therefore $S_r\mapsto S'_r$ is a bijection of this subgroup. It depends only on $r$ and $z$, so the $S'_r$ remain independent and uniform conditional on $\mathcal A$.

Run the available-set and mass recursion with these replaced switches, starting from the same $(B_0,p_0)$. We claim that at stage $r$ its state is $(T_{z_r}B_r,p_r\circ T_{z_r}^{-1})$. This holds initially because $T_{z_0}$ is the identity. Conjugation carries the matching edges at the previous stage bijectively to matching edges. An edge with two available endpoints is averaged in either recursion, and a subsequent fixed swap does not change its equal output masses. An edge with one available endpoint carries its mass to the image of that endpoint under the replaced switch. The assertion is immediate on edges with no available endpoint. These three cases prove the induction. Centering by the uniform mass on the available set transforms in the same way. Since the two switch arrays have the same conditional law, the terminal invariance follows.

Every intermediate switch preserves $x_i$, proving the assertion about the counts. The squared-energy decrease in the final coordinate-$i$ layer is $$L(B,a)=\frac12
 \sum_{\substack{\{x,y\}\subseteq B\\x+y=e_i}}
                 (a(x)-a(y))^2,
 \qquad (B,a)=(B_{d-1},a_{d-1}).$$ Indeed, transport preserves squared norm and averaging two entries decreases it by half the square of their difference. We may average this expression after applying an independent uniform $T_z$, by the conditional invariance already proved. Each pair $x\in B\cap H_0$, $y\in B\cap H_1$ becomes a coordinate-$i$ pair for exactly one of the $N/2$ possible values of $z$. Consequently $$\mathbb E_z L(T_zB,a\circ T_z^{-1})
 =\frac1N\sum_{x\in B\cap H_0,\ y\in B\cap H_1}
                         (a(x)-a(y))^2.$$ The entries of $a$ sum to zero. With $A=\sum_{x\in B\cap H_0}a(x)$, the last double sum equals $$h_1\sum_{B\cap H_0}a(x)^2+
 h_0\sum_{B\cap H_1}a(y)^2+2A^2
 \ge\min(h_0,h_1)E_{d-1}.$$ Taking conditional expectations proves <a href="#shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-loss">[shear:eq:delayed-symmetry-loss]</a>. Empty halves give a zero lower bound and require no separate division. When $d=1$, the same calculation has only $z=0$ and remains valid. ◻

</details>

In a hidden-card application, $B_0$ and $p_0$ are the available set and its conditional mass vector at the conditioning time. They are functions of the observed path prefix. Conditioning additionally on all past switches fixes these two functions; it does not redefine $p_0$ as a conditional law given every card position. Future matching coins are still independent and fair, so the lemma applies to the stated transport-and-averaging recursion. If the coordinate schedule is any periodic permutation of the $d$ coordinates, take $\mathcal A$ immediately after the previous visit to $i$ and apply the lemma to the intervening $d-1$ layers. Reversing the coordinate order is included in the same statement.

<a id="shear:sec:completion"></a>
### Fourier amplification of partial permutation laws

The marginal estimates now provide two routes to coset information. A bound for every fixed input list applies to each prescribed partition whose complementary lists meet the stated length condition. An averaged input bound first requires the choice of one partition. In either case the Fourier argument uses one common modification of the permutation law whose relevant coset masses are bounded. We first state that modification and the transfer estimates, then apply them to the preceding marginal bounds.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### From complementary marginals to one retained law

Let $X$ be a finite label set, $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(X)$, and let $M_1,\ldots,M_r$ be a partition of $X$ into nonempty parts. Write $H_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(M_i)$ for the subgroup fixing $X\setminus M_i$ pointwise. Two label-to-position permutations agree on $X\setminus M_i$ exactly when they lie in the same left coset $gH_i$. Thus the complementary image tuple identifies that coset, and its distance from a uniform injection is the distance of the coset law from uniform on $G/H_i$. Write $\delta_i$ for this distance and put $\delta=\sum_i\delta_i$.

If a marginal estimate averages over omitted sets of size $|X|/r$, sample a uniform ordered partition into $r$ equal parts. Each part has the required uniform subset law, so the expected sum of its complementary errors is $r$ times the mean single-subset error. Some deterministic partition has a sum no larger than this expectation. The ordering chosen on a complementary set does not affect its error, because changing that ordering permutes the tuple coordinates. Thus an average over uniform ordered input tuples is also the corresponding average over complementary sets.

For the representation estimates, write $\rho_\lambda$ for the symmetric-group irreducible indexed by $\lambda$ and $D_\lambda=f^\lambda=\dim\rho_\lambda$ for its degree. For $u>0$, define $$C_u=\sup_{s\ge1}\sum_{\lambda\vdash s}(f^\lambda)^{-u},
 \qquad
 Z_u(s)=\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash s\\
                   \lambda\notin\{(s),(1^s)\}}}(f^\lambda)^{-u}.$$ The exclusions form a set, so the sole partition of $1$ is removed once.

<a id="stmt-shear-prop-fourier-inputs"></a>
<div id="shear:prop:fourier-inputs" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (Degree and coset inputs). *For every fixed $u>0$, $C_u<\infty$ and $Z_u(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$.*

*Let $H_1,\ldots,H_r$ be subgroups of a finite group $G$, and let $\mu$ be a probability measure. Write $\mu_H$ for its image on $G/H$. If $$\delta=\sum_{i=1}^r
 \|\mu_{H_i}-U_{G/H_i}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},$$ there is a measure $0\le f\le\mu$, of mass $z\ge1-\delta$, with $$f(gH_i)\le [G:H_i]^{-1}\qquad(g\in G,\ 1\le i\le r).$$ It can be obtained by successive trimming of excess coset mass or by taking the pointwise minimum of the separately trimmed measures. When $z>0$, its normalization is within $1-z$ of $\mu$ in TV and has coset cap $z^{-1}$ times uniform.*

*Alternatively, delete the union of all cosets whose original $\mu$-mass exceeds twice uniform. The deleted mass is $\eta\le2\delta$. If $\delta<1/2$, the normalized retained law is exactly $\eta$ away from $\mu$ in TV and has coset cap $2/(1-\eta)$ times uniform.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-prop-fourier-inputs"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-prop-fourier-inputs</summary>

*Proof.* The degree assertions follow from <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Theorem l:degree-all-powers]. The same companion gives separate proofs at powers $1,2,10$ in Lemmas , , and ; these also justify the fixed-power sums used below. The two modifications, including their common-measure and mass claims, follow from <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Lemma l:trim]. The normalization assertion for $f$ follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:subprobability-fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:subprobability-fourier">29</a> below; dividing the exact cap by $z$ gives the normalized cap. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Fourier bounds for disjoint supports

For a nonnegative mass function $f$ on $G$, use $$\widehat f(\rho)=\sum_{g\in G}f(g)\rho(g).$$ The Hilbert–Schmidt norm is unnormalized: $\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*A)$. The next proposition extends the companion’s partition estimates to disjoint supports that may leave labels unused. The common restriction decomposition has two uses: assigning each product type to one small factor controls the orbit of a vector, while overlapping central projections control the whole Fourier matrix.

<a id="stmt-shear-prop-disjoint-transfer"></a>
<div id="shear:prop:disjoint-transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:noncovering-isotypic-general"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:orbit-bound"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:hs-general"></a>
<a id="shear:eq:hs-branching"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Fourier bounds from disjoint coset systems). *Let $X$ have $N$ elements and $G=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(X)$. Let $M_1,\ldots,M_r$ be disjoint nonempty subsets of $X$, where $r\ge1$, and put $H_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(M_i)$, fixing the complement pointwise. Their union need not be $X$. Suppose $f\ge0$ has mass $z\le1$ and $$f(gH_i)\le K[G:H_i]^{-1}\qquad(g\in G,\ 1\le i\le r)$$ for some $K\ge0$. For an irreducible unitary representation $\rho$ of degree $D$, set $$S_i(D)=
 \sum_{\substack{\tau\in\widehat H_i\\ \dim\tau\le D^{1/r}}}
       (\dim\tau)^2.$$ For every $u>0$, the separate orbit and isotypic estimates are $$\begin{aligned}
 \|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 &\le \left(\frac{zK}{D}\sum_iS_i(D)\right)^{1/2}
 \le \sqrt{zKrC_u}\,D^{-1/2+(u+2)/(2r)},
 \label{shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general}\\
 \|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 &\le \frac{zK}{D}\sum_iS_i(D)
 \le zKrC_uD^{-1+(u+2)/r}.
 \label{shear:eq:noncovering-isotypic-general}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, when $r>4$, the inverse-square orbit estimate gives $$\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le\sqrt{rKC_2}\,D^{-(1-4/r)/2}.
 \label{shear:eq:orbit-bound}$$ A separate coefficient-projection argument gives $$\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le\frac{K^2}{D}\sum_{i=1}^r
       \sum_{\substack{\tau\in\widehat H_i\\
                            \dim\tau\le D^{1/r}}}(\dim\tau)^2
 \le K^2rC_{10}D^{-1+12/r}.
 \label{shear:eq:hs-general}$$ Finally, for $\rho=\rho_\lambda$ with $\lambda\vdash N$, put $$k_\lambda=N-\max(\lambda_1,\lambda'_1),
 \qquad M(k)=\sum_{j=0}^k p(j),$$ where $p(j)$ is the partition number and $p(0)=1$. The branching refinement is $$\|\widehat f(\rho_\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le zKrM(k_\lambda)D^{-1+2/r}.
 \label{shear:eq:hs-branching}$$ All these statements allow unequal support sizes and unrestricted restriction multiplicities. In particular they include the probability-law cases $z=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-prop-disjoint-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-prop-disjoint-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:disjoint-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:disjoint-transfer">28</a>.* Write $A=\widehat f(\rho)$ and abbreviate $S_i(D)$ to $S_i$. If $z=0$, then $f=0$ and every assertion is immediate. Assume $z>0$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### The common small-type cover.

Disjoint supports embed $H_1\times\cdots\times H_r$ in $G$: the factors commute, and a product that is identity restricts to the identity on every $M_i$. Complete reducibility and the direct-product description of irreducibles <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[etingof](#ref-etingof), Theorems 3.12 and 4.25 and Proposition 3.14] give $$V_\rho\big|_{H_1\times\cdots\times H_r}
 =
 \bigoplus_{\boldsymbol\tau}
 (V_{\tau_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\tau_r})
       \otimes\mathcal A_{\boldsymbol\tau}.$$ The sum is over distinct occurring product types, with their full multiplicity spaces. Each has $\prod_i\dim\tau_i\le D$, so some factor has degree at most $D^{1/r}$. Unused labels can change the occurring types and their multiplicities, but do not change this product bound.

<a id="shear:eq:disjoint-projector-cover"></a>
Let $P_{i,\tau}$ be the orthogonal projection onto all copies of the $H_i$-type $\tau$, and put $$P_i=\sum_{\substack{\tau\in\widehat H_i\\
                         \dim\tau\le D^{1/r}}}P_{i,\tau},
 \qquad Q_i=P_i\prod_{j<i}(I-P_j).$$ On each product-type summand, $P_i$ is identity or zero according as the $i$th factor is small or large. Hence the $P_i$ commute, and at least one is identity on each summand. Therefore $$I\preceq\sum_{i=1}^rP_i,\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^rQ_i=I,\qquad Q_iQ_j=0\quad(i\ne j).
 \label{shear:eq:disjoint-projector-cover}$$ The $Q_i$ assign a whole product type to its first small factor; the $P_i$ may overlap. Both families retain every multiplicity. For every $u>0$, the definition of $C_u$ gives $$S_i\le D^{(u+2)/r}
       \sum_{\tau\in\widehat H_i}(\dim\tau)^{-u}
 \le C_uD^{(u+2)/r}.$$

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### The orbit estimate.

For $v\in V_\rho$, write $v_i=Q_iv$ and $W_i=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{\rho(h)v_i:h\in H_i\}$. The single-vector orbit lemma <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Lemma l:single-orbit] gives $\dim W_i\le S_i$. Indeed, in all copies of a type $\tau$, the orbit of its component is contained in the image of $$\operatorname{End}(V_\tau)\longrightarrow
 V_\tau\otimes\mathcal A_\tau,\qquad
 T\longmapsto(T\otimes I)v_{i,\tau},$$ whose dimension is at most $(\dim\tau)^2$, independently of the multiplicity.

Let $\Pi_i$ project onto $W_i$. Since $W_i$ is $H_i$-invariant, the projection onto $\rho(g)W_i$ depends only on $gH_i$. The coset cap and Schur averaging yield $$\sum_g f(g)\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_i}w\|^2
 \le K\frac{\dim W_i}{D}\|w\|^2.$$ Here the uniform average of $\rho(g)\Pi_i\rho(g)^*$ is $(\dim W_i/D)I$, by its trace and irreducibility. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz retains the mass $z$: $$|\langle w,Av_i\rangle|^2
 \le z\|v_i\|^2\sum_g f(g)\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_i}w\|^2
 \le\frac{zKS_i}{D}\|w\|^2\|v_i\|^2.$$ The $v_i$ are orthogonal. Summing and applying Cauchy–Schwarz over $i$ gives $$|\langle w,Av\rangle|
 \le\left(\frac{zK}{D}\sum_iS_i\right)^{1/2}\|w\|\|v\|.$$ This proves <a href="#shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general">[shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general]</a>. Its specialization $u=2$, with $z\le1$, is <a href="#shear:eq:orbit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:orbit-bound">[shear:eq:orbit-bound]</a>; the condition $r>4$ makes that dimension exponent negative.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### The isotypic estimate.

<a id="shear:eq:noncovering-central"></a>
For a subgroup $H\le G$ and an irreducible $H$-type $\tau$ of degree $a$, define $$e_{H,\tau}(h)=\frac{a}{|H|}\overline{\chi_\tau(h)}
 \quad(h\in H),\qquad e_{H,\tau}=0\quad\text{off }H.$$ Its transform is the projection $P_{\sigma,H,\tau}$ onto all copies of $\tau$ in $\sigma|_H$. Thus $\widehat{f*e_{H,\tau}}(\sigma)=
\widehat f(\sigma)P_{\sigma,H,\tau}$: a bound on the left cosets $gH$ controls a projection on the right of the Fourier matrix. The companion’s cosetwise isotypic lemma <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Lemma l:central-isotypic] applies to every subgroup $H$. If $f(gH)\le K[G:H]^{-1}$, it gives $$\sum_{\sigma\in\widehat G}D_\sigma
       \|\widehat f(\sigma)P_{\sigma,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =|G|\sum_g|(f*e_{H,\tau})(g)|^2\le Kza^2.
 \label{shear:eq:noncovering-central}$$ This is the central-projection step; it retains the whole projected Fourier matrix and the mass factor.

Trace the first inequality of <a href="#shear:eq:disjoint-projector-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:disjoint-projector-cover">[shear:eq:disjoint-projector-cover]</a> against $A^*A$, and apply <a href="#shear:eq:noncovering-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:noncovering-central">[shear:eq:noncovering-central]</a> to each selected $H_i$-type after discarding the other ambient irreducibles. Then $$D\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le D\sum_i\sum_{\dim\tau\le D^{1/r}}
       \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*AP_{i,\tau})
 =D\sum_i\sum_{\dim\tau\le D^{1/r}}
       \|AP_{i,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le Kz\sum_iS_i.$$ The trace inequality uses positivity of $A(\sum_iP_i-I)A^*$; it does not require $A^*A$ to commute with the projections. This proves <a href="#shear:eq:noncovering-isotypic-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:noncovering-isotypic-general">[shear:eq:noncovering-isotypic-general]</a> using the overlapping $P_i$, rather than the orbit assignment $Q_i$.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### The coefficient alternative.

The independent coefficient calculation in <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Lemma l:isotypic-alternatives] bounds each coset separately. Its norm and orientation give the selected-type refinement in <a href="#shear:eq:hs-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:hs-general">[shear:eq:hs-general]</a>. For one subgroup $H\le G$, put $F=|G|f$, so $\mathbb E_{h\sim U_H}F(xh)=[G:H]f(xH)\le K$. For an $H$-type $\tau$ of degree $a$, choose one representative $x$ of each coset and set $$T_x=\mathbb E_{h\sim U_H}F(xh)\tau(h),\qquad
 q(xh)=a\sum_{j,\ell=1}^a(T_x)_{j\ell}\overline{\tau(h)_{j\ell}}.$$ The function $q$ on this coset is the orthogonal projection of $F$ onto the conjugate coefficient space of $\tau$. Schur orthogonality and the nonnegative weights $F(xh)$ give <a id="shear:eq:coset-coefficient-norm"></a>$$\mathbb E_{h\sim U_H}|q(xh)|^2
 =a\|T_x\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le a^2\|T_x\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
 \le a^2\bigl(\mathbb E_{h\sim U_H}F(xh)\bigr)^2
 \le K^2a^2.
 \label{shear:eq:coset-coefficient-norm}$$ Expanding $T_x$ and using, for $h,k\in H$, $\overline{\chi_\tau(k^{-1}h)}=\chi_\tau(kh^{-1})$ shows that these coset projections form the single function $q=|G|(f*e_{H,\tau})$. Thus their tested Fourier matrix is $\widehat f(\sigma)P_{\sigma,H,\tau}$, with the same right-hand orientation as above. Averaging the preceding norm bound over cosets and applying complex finite-group Plancherel gives <a id="shear:eq:coefficient-one-type"></a>$$\sum_{\sigma\in\widehat G}D_\sigma
       \|\widehat f(\sigma)P_{\sigma,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\mathbb E_{g\sim U_G}|q(g)|^2\le K^2a^2.
 \label{shear:eq:coefficient-one-type}$$ The calculation counts a type once, regardless of its multiplicity, and assumes no pointwise bound inside the coset. The same positive trace comparison now gives $\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le K^2D^{-1}\sum_iS_i$. Using $S_i\le C_{10}D^{12/r}$ proves <a href="#shear:eq:hs-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:hs-general">[shear:eq:hs-general]</a>, including its original sum over the types of the unmodified subgroups $H_i$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### The branching refinement.

Each $H_i$ is conjugate to the standard subgroup $S_{|M_i|}$ of $S_N$. Young branching <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[James1978](#ref-James1978), Theorem 9.2] shows that every occurring type $\tau\vdash |M_i|$ is a subdiagram of $\lambda$; different corner-removal sequences can give arbitrary multiplicity. If $\lambda_1\ge\lambda'_1$, the diagram $\tau$ has at most $k_\lambda$ boxes below its first row. Its lower diagram, together with its fixed size, determines the first row, so at most $M(k_\lambda)$ distinct types can occur. If $\lambda'_1>\lambda_1$, transpose both diagrams for this count. Transposition preserves degrees and does not replace $f$ or its Fourier matrix by a sign-twisted law.

Nonoccurring isotypic projections are zero. In the positive trace comparison, apply <a href="#shear:eq:noncovering-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:noncovering-central">[shear:eq:noncovering-central]</a> only to the occurring selected types and use $(\dim\tau)^2\le D^{2/r}$. There are at most $M(k_\lambda)$ of them for each support, giving <a href="#shear:eq:hs-branching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:hs-branching">[shear:eq:hs-branching]</a>. The same argument includes $k_\lambda=0$, where the representation is trivial or sign, $D=1$, and $M(0)=1$. It also includes all unequal nonempty supports and every restriction multiplicity. ◻

</details>

The isotypic estimate has the same dimension exponent as the squared orbit estimate, but bounds the whole Hilbert–Schmidt norm. The coefficient estimate records a different proof of a bound with cap factor $K^2$. The branching estimate counts only types that can occur in a fixed ambient diagram. These methods use only coset masses; none requires a pointwise bound on the conditional distribution inside a coset.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Normalization and independent time blocks

We record the mass normalization once for all the applications. The Fourier and sign conventions are those of the completed local proof.

<a id="stmt-lem-subprobability-fourier"></a>
<div id="lem:subprobability-fourier" class="lemma">

<a id="shear:eq:plancherel-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:subprobability-full"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Fourier normalization for retained mass). *For a complex mass function $a$ on a finite group $G$, $$\sum_\rho D_\rho\|\widehat a(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =|G|\sum_g|a(g)|^2.
 \label{shear:eq:plancherel-mass}$$ If $\xi\ge0$ has mass $m\le1$, its transform is not divided by $m$, and <a id="eq:subprobability-centered"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |G|\sum_g\left|\xi(g)-\frac{m}{|G|}\right|^2
 &=\sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv}}D_\rho
                   \|\widehat\xi(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2,
 \label{eq:subprobability-centered}\\
 \left(\sum_g|\xi(g)-U_G(g)|\right)^2
 &\le(1-m)^2+\sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv}}D_\rho
                   \|\widehat\xi(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
 \label{eq:subprobability-full}
\end{aligned}$$ If $\xi\le\mu$ for a probability measure $\mu$, then $\sum_g|\mu(g)-\xi(g)|=1-m$. When $m>0$, $\|\xi/m-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\le1-m$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-subprobability-fourier"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-subprobability-fourier</summary>

*Proof.* The regular-character expansion in Lemma <a href="#lem:plancherel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plancherel">14</a>, with coefficients $a(g)\overline{a(h)}$, proves <a href="#shear:eq:plancherel-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:plancherel-mass">[shear:eq:plancherel-mass]</a>. Apply it to $\xi-mU_G$ and $\xi-U_G$. Their trivial coefficients are respectively $0$ and $m-1$, and their nontrivial coefficients are those of $\xi$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives <a href="#eq:subprobability-full" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subprobability-full">[eq:subprobability-full]</a>. If $\xi\le\mu$, the nonnegative difference has mass $1-m$. Also $\sum_g|\xi(g)/m-\xi(g)|=1-m$; the triangle inequality and the factor $1/2$ in TV prove the normalization claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="shear:eq:subprob-plancherel"></a>
For a subprobability $f$ of mass $z$ and a positive integer $\ell$, the same identity and the convolution rule give $$|G|\sum_g|f^{*\ell}(g)-|G|^{-1}|^2
 =(z^\ell-1)^2+
 \sum_{\rho\ne\mathrm{triv}}D_\rho
             \|\widehat f(\rho)^\ell\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
 \label{shear:eq:subprob-plancherel}$$ One half of the square root bounds one half of the $\ell^1$ distance from $f^{*\ell}$ to $U_G$; for a probability this is TV. The matrix bounds used below are $$\|A^\ell\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\sqrt D\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{\ell},
 \qquad
 \|A^\ell\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\ell}.$$ They require no normality assumption.

<a id="shear:eq:parity"></a>
For every positive-time physical block law $\mu$, Lemma <a href="#lem:fair-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fair-sign">15</a> gives $\widehat\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})=0$. If a probability $\nu$ is within $\eta$ of $\mu$ in TV, then $$|\widehat\nu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|\le2\eta.
 \label{shear:eq:parity}$$ If instead $0\le f\le\mu$ has mass $z$, then $|\widehat f(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|\le1-z$. Positivity also gives $f^{*\ell}\le\mu^{*\ell}$, with missing mass $1-z^\ell$. For nearby probabilities, replacing the $\ell$ factors one at a time costs at most $\ell\eta$ in TV.

Every block length below is a multiple of $d$. Thus $R^T=I$ in <a href="#eq:frames:cyclic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frames:cyclic">[eq:frames:cyclic]</a>, and the coordinate block $\mathcal L(X_T)$ is exactly the physical law $q_d^{*T}$. Independent time blocks combine by convolution.

<a id="stmt-shear-cor-512"></a>
<div id="shear:cor:512" class="corollary">

**Corollary 30** (Two time blocks from the isotypic transfer). *For the Thorp shuffle on $N=2^d$ cards, $$\|q_d^{*(512d)}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(d\longrightarrow\infty).$$ The convergence is uniform over deterministic initial orderings.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-cor-512"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-cor-512</summary>

*Proof.* Let $T=256d$ and $\mu=q_d^{*T}$. For $d\ge3$, partition the labels into eight sets $M_i$ of size $N/8$. The fixed-list estimate <a href="#shear:eq:256-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:256-marginal">[shear:eq:256-marginal]</a> bounds each complementary image-tuple error by $$\delta_N=\frac{N^{3/2}}2
 \left((31/32)^{255d}+64e^{-N/64}\right)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ Exact trimming by Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:fourier-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:fourier-inputs">27</a> gives $0\le f\le\mu$ with mass $z$, where $1-z\le8\delta_N=o(1)$, and cap one on all eight coset systems.

For this covering partition, the companion isotypic estimate <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Proposition l:isotypic], with inverse-degree parameter $1$, gives $\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le8zC_1D^{-5/8}\le8C_1D^{-5/8}$. Consequently $$\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash N\\
                  \lambda\notin\{(N),(1^N)\}}}
 D_\lambda\|\widehat f(\rho_\lambda)^2\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le(8C_1)^2Z_{1/4}(N)=o(1),$$ since $1-2(5/8)=-1/4$ and <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Theorem l:degree-all-powers] gives the inverse-quarter limit. The sign coefficient of $f$ is at most $1-z$ in magnitude, and the trivial coefficient of $f^{*2}$ is $z^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:subprobability-fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:subprobability-fourier">29</a> therefore gives $$\left(\sum_g|f^{*2}(g)-U_G(g)|\right)^2
 \le(1-z^2)^2+(1-z)^4+(8C_1)^2Z_{1/4}(N)=o(1).$$ Adding back the missing mass $1-z^2=o(1)$ proves $\|\mu^{*2}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1)$. The two physical blocks take $2T=512d$ shuffles, and <a href="#eq:worst-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:worst-state">[eq:worst-state]</a> gives uniformity over starts. ◻

</details>

The orbit, coefficient, and branching estimates give the following separate applications, with different modifications of the law.

<a id="stmt-shear-thm-2048"></a>
<div id="shear:thm:2048" class="theorem">

**Theorem 31** (Eight time blocks from the orbit estimate). *For the physical shuffle on $N=2^d$ labeled cards, <a id="shear:eq:2048"></a>$$\|q_d^{*(2048d)}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).
 \label{shear:eq:2048}$$ The fixed-list estimate at $257d$ also gives a proof using eight time blocks at $2056d$ with a retained subprobability. The random omitted set estimate gives another proof at $2056d$ using normalized deletion. All three conclusions hold for every deterministic starting deck.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-thm-2048"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-thm-2048</summary>

*Proof.* For $T=256d$, let $\mu=q_d^{*T}$ and use eight equal label parts. The error $\delta_N$ in the preceding proof bounds each complementary marginal. Deleting heavy cosets loses $\eta\le16\delta_N=o(1)$ and produces a probability $\nu$ with cap $K\le4$ for large $d$. Equation <a href="#shear:eq:orbit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:orbit-bound">[shear:eq:orbit-bound]</a> with $r=8$ gives $$\|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le C D^{-1/4},\qquad C=\sqrt{32C_2}.$$ For eight factors the nonsign, nontrivial Plancherel contribution is at most $$C^{16}\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash N\\
                        \lambda\notin\{(N),(1^N)\}}}
 D_\lambda^{\,2-16/4}=C^{16}Z_2(N)=o(1).$$ The sign contribution is at most $(2\eta)^{16}$, and the trivial coefficient is one. Thus $\nu^{*8}$ tends to uniform in TV. Restoring the true factors costs at most $8\eta=o(1)$, and $8T=2048d$ physical shuffles.

For the fixed-list subprobability route take $T=257d$. Equation <a href="#shear:eq:257-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:257-marginal">[shear:eq:257-marginal]</a> makes the summed error on any partition into eight equal parts $o(1)$. Exact trimming gives $f\le\mu=q_d^{*T}$ of mass $z=1-o(1)$ and cap one. The orbit bound is $\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{8C_2}D^{-1/4}$. In <a href="#shear:eq:subprob-plancherel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:subprob-plancherel">[shear:eq:subprob-plancherel]</a>, the trivial term is $(z^8-1)^2$, the sign term is at most $(1-z)^{16}$, and the remaining sum is at most $(8C_2)^8Z_2(N)$. These terms and the restored mass $1-z^8$ all vanish. The eight time blocks take $2056d$ physical shuffles.

For the random omitted set route, Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:reservoir" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:reservoir">21</a> and the partition averaging above give a partition into eight equal parts with summed error $o(1)$ at $T=257d$. Heavy-coset deletion loses $\eta=o(1)$ and gives cap $K=2/(1-\eta)$. The orbit bound is $\sqrt{16C_2/(1-\eta)}D^{-1/4}$. The eight-factor nonsign sum is at most $[16C_2/(1-\eta)]^8Z_2(N)=o(1)$, the sign term is at most $(2\eta)^{16}$, and restoring the true factors costs $8\eta=o(1)$. Again $8T=2056d$. Equation <a href="#eq:worst-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:worst-state">[eq:worst-state]</a> handles every start. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-frames-average-marginals"></a>
<div id="frames:average-marginals" class="proposition">

**Proposition 32** (Sixteen time blocks from averaged input lists). *Let $d\ge6$, $b=64$, $q=N(1-1/b)$, $T=2048d$, and $\mu_T=q_d^{*T}$. The average over uniform ordered lists of $q$ distinct input labels of the total-variation distance of their outputs from a uniform injection is $o(1)$. There is a subprobability $f\le\mu_T$ of mass $1-o(1)$ and a partition into 64 equal parts for which every complementary marginal of $f$ is bounded above by the corresponding uniform marginal. Consequently, $$\|q_d^{*(32768d)}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$ The full-deck conclusion holds for every deterministic starting deck.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frames-average-marginals"><summary>Proof of stmt-frames-average-marginals</summary>

*Proof.* The ordinary marginal assertion is Lemma <a href="#frames:average-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frames:average-law">25</a>, specifically <a href="#frames:average-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frames:average-tv">[frames:average-tv]</a>. The partition averaging above selects 64 equal parts whose complementary errors sum to $o(1)$. Exact trimming by Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:fourier-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:fourier-inputs">27</a> gives $0\le f\le\mu_T$, of mass $z=1-o(1)$, with cap one for all 64 coset systems. This is the stated domination of the complementary marginals.

Use the direct inverse-tenth input cited in Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:fourier-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:fourier-inputs">27</a> and the orbit estimate <a href="#shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general">[shear:eq:noncovering-orbit-general]</a> with $r=64$, $u=10$, and $z\le1$. It gives $$\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le\sqrt{64C_{10}}D^{-13/32},
 \qquad -\frac12+\frac{12}{128}=-\frac{13}{32}.$$ At sixteen factors the nonsign, nontrivial Plancherel sum is at most $$(64C_{10})^{16}
 \sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash N\\
                  \lambda\notin\{(N),(1^N)\}}}
 D_\lambda^{\,2-32(13/32)}
 \le(64C_{10})^{16}Z_{10}(N)=o(1),$$ because the displayed exponent is $-11$. The sign contribution is at most $(1-z)^{32}$, and the trivial term is $(z^{16}-1)^2$. Equation <a href="#shear:eq:subprob-plancherel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:subprob-plancherel">[shear:eq:subprob-plancherel]</a> and restoration of the missing mass $1-z^{16}=o(1)$ prove convergence for $\mu_T^{*16}$. The block length $T=2048d$ is a multiple of $d$, so these are $16T=32768d$ physical shuffles. Equation <a href="#eq:worst-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:worst-state">[eq:worst-state]</a> gives the assertion from every deterministic start. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shear-thm-32-block"></a>
<div id="shear:thm:32-block" class="theorem">

**Theorem 33** (Thirty-two time blocks from coefficient projection). *The fixed-list marginal bound at $T=2049d$ has a separate Hilbert–Schmidt amplification proving $$\|q_d^{*(65568d)}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-thm-32-block"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-thm-32-block</summary>

*Proof.* Use 32 equal label parts. Equation <a href="#shear:eq:2049-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:2049-marginal">[shear:eq:2049-marginal]</a> makes all complementary errors $o(1)$. Exact trimming gives $f\le\mu=q_d^{*T}$ of mass $z=1-o(1)$. Its normalization $\nu=f/z$ is within $1-z$ of $\mu$ in TV and has cap at most two for large $d$. The coefficient estimate <a href="#shear:eq:hs-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:hs-general">[shear:eq:hs-general]</a>, with $r=32$ and $K=2$, gives $$\|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le C'D^{-5/8},\qquad C'=128C_{10}.$$ For 32 factors the nonsign, nontrivial Plancherel sum is at most $$(C')^{32}\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash N\\
                           \lambda\notin\{(N),(1^N)\}}}D_\lambda^{-19}
 \le(C')^{32}Z_{10}(N)=o(1).$$ The sign term is at most $[2(1-z)]^{64}$, and the trivial coefficient is one. Restoring the true factors costs $32(1-z)=o(1)$. The physical time is $32T=32(2049d)=65568d$; translation invariance gives the same conclusion from every deterministic start. ◻

</details>

<a id="shear:eq:type-absorption"></a>
For the last application we use the companion’s eventual type-count bound. For every fixed integer $r\ge1$, uniformly over $\lambda\vdash N$ with $k_\lambda\ge1$, all sufficiently large $N$ satisfy $$4rM(k_\lambda)\le D_\lambda^{1/4}.
 \label{shear:eq:type-absorption}$$ This is <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[partial-fourier](#ref-partial-fourier), Lemma l:degree-type-absorption].

<a id="stmt-shear-thm-4096"></a>
<div id="shear:thm:4096" class="theorem">

**Theorem 34** (Four time blocks from the branching estimate). *The averaged-input estimate of Proposition <a href="#shear:prop:random-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:prop:random-tuple">24</a> has an amplification proving $$\|q_d^{*(4096d)}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-thm-4096"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-thm-4096</summary>

*Proof.* At $T=1024d$, choose eight equal label parts whose summed complementary error is $o(1)$. Heavy-coset deletion produces a probability $\nu$ within $\eta=o(1)$ of $\mu=q_d^{*T}$, with cap at most four. For every $\lambda\notin\{(N),(1^N)\}$, the branching estimate and <a href="#shear:eq:type-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:type-absorption">[shear:eq:type-absorption]</a>, with $r=8$, give $$\|\widehat\nu(\rho_\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le4rM(k_\lambda)D_\lambda^{-1+2/r}
 \le D_\lambda^{-1/2}.$$ Four factors therefore leave the inverse-first Plancherel sum: $$4\|\nu^{*4}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^2
 \le(2\eta)^8+
       \sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash N\\k_\lambda\ge1}}D_\lambda^{-1}
 =(2\eta)^8+Z_1(N)=o(1).$$ The displayed sign term uses <a href="#shear:eq:parity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shear:eq:parity">[shear:eq:parity]</a>; the trivial coefficient is one. Restoring the four true factors costs $4\eta=o(1)$. Their physical time is $4T=4096d$, and <a href="#eq:worst-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:worst-state">[eq:worst-state]</a> gives the same conclusion from every start. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shear-cor-order"></a>
<div id="shear:cor:order" class="corollary">

**Corollary 35** (Mixing order and lower estimates). *For all sufficiently large $d$, $$d<t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)\le512d.$$ Thus the mixing time has order $\Theta(d)$ in physical shuffles. The one-card support bound also gives $t_{\mathrm{mix}}(d)\ge d$ for every $d\ge1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shear-cor-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-shear-cor-order</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lower">2</a> gives distance at least $1-(e/\sqrt N)^N>1/4$ for every integer $t\le d$ when $d$ is large. Lemma <a href="#lem:one-card-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-card-lower">3</a> gives the stated one-card bound for every $d$. The upper bound follows from Corollary <a href="#shear:cor:512" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:cor:512">30</a>; <a href="#eq:sharp-support-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-support-time">[eq:sharp-support-time]</a> gives the sharper lower asymptotic $2d-O(1)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="frames:chapter"></a>
### History events and conditional estimates

The preceding sections obtained marginal estimates both for every fixed input list and after averaging the list. We now retain information about trajectory histories before passing to endpoint laws. For a fixed partition and fixed orders on its complements, one event gives pointwise coset caps after its energy constraints are removed in descending order. A stopped estimate for a fixed list charges imbalance only once across the list. A third construction gives an entropy comparison for every probability law supported on one common event of cyclic histories.

The frame comparison, the balance estimate, and the retained conclusion play separate roles in these arguments. In particular, the deferred shear frame used in the final application preserves the distance for each fixed input list; that application averages the labels to obtain simultaneous balance across affine half-spaces. We give the three history constructions and their full-deck consequences first, then return to that averaged application.

Throughout this section $n=2^d$, $V=\mathbb F_2^d$, and $G=\operatorname{Sym}(V)$. Permutations send input positions to output positions and multiply by composition. Write $U_G$ for uniform measure. For $v\ne0$, retain the matching subgroup $C_v$: it consists of the permutations preserving every pair $\{x,x+v\}$ setwise. A uniform element of $C_v$ is a layer of independent fair switches. Removing the accumulated coordinate rotation from the physical shuffle gives the cyclic directions $w_t=e_{1+((t-1)\bmod d)}$. In particular a block of length divisible by $d$ has exactly the same endpoint permutation in these coordinates as in physical coordinates.

<a id="stmt-frames-lower-import"></a>
<div id="frames:lower-import" class="remark">

*Remark 36*. Every upper bound in this section is paired with the support estimate of Lemma <a href="#lem:lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lower">2</a>: there are at most $2^{tn/2}$ outcomes after $t$ physical shuffles, so distance from uniform is at least $1-2^{tn/2}/n!$. For integer $t\le d$ this tends uniformly to one. Consequently each upper bound below has order $d$, and the mixing time at threshold $1/4$ is at least $d$ for sufficiently large $d$.

</div>

<a id="frames:invariance-section"></a>
#### Preserving the matching of labels

<a id="stmt-frames-matching-invariance"></a>
<div id="frames:matching-invariance" class="lemma">

**Lemma 37** (Matching and input-list invariance). *Fix an integer $T\ge d$ and directions $v_1,\ldots,v_T$ such that every consecutive $d$ form a basis. There are invertible linear maps $L_0,\ldots,L_T$ with $L_0e_i=v_i$ for $1\le i\le d$ such that, if $g_t$ is the cyclic-layer process, then $L_tg_tL_0^{-1}$ is the independent switch process with directions $v_t$. This assertion is an equality in law conditional on the entire fixed sequence. If $L_0=I$, the matching on trajectory labels before each switch is unchanged. For general $L_0$, the same statement holds after relabeling the initial labels by $L_0$. Consequently the average, over uniform ordered input lists of any fixed length, of the endpoint distance to a uniform injection is unchanged.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frames-matching-invariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-frames-matching-invariance</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:general-frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:general-frame">4</a> gives exactly the asserted path-law identity, with $L_0e_i=v_i$. Its construction applies to every fixed schedule with consecutive basis windows and permits an arbitrary initial basis. It remains to check what happens to trajectory labels.

If labels $a,b$ are paired in the cyclic process just before layer $t$, then $g_{t-1}(b)=g_{t-1}(a)+w_t$. The column identity in that frame construction is $L_{t-1}w_t=v_t$. Hence their transformed positions, with initial labels changed by $L_0$, differ by $v_t$ and are paired in the transformed process. The implication reverses because $L_{t-1}$ is invertible. When $L_0=I$, the labels themselves do not change. For general $L_0$, its bijection preserves the uniform law of the input list. The terminal bijection $L_T$ preserves uniform injections and total variation. This proves both assertions. ◻

</details>

The maps $L_t$ may depend on future directions. We use them only after fixing the complete schedule. In the probabilistic calculations below, the conditional switch law given that schedule is the product of the uniform matching laws. We reveal directions chronologically and average them only in the prefix fields specified below.

<a id="stmt-frames-holes"></a>
<div id="frames:holes" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38** (Conditional mass on unobserved positions). *Fix an integer $T\ge1$ and nonzero directions $v_1,\ldots,v_T$. Let $Z_0=I$ and $Z_t=\xi_tZ_{t-1}$, where the $\xi_t$ are independent uniform switches in directions $v_t$. Fix complete feasible trajectories of some observed labels. If $m\ge1$ labels remain unobserved, let $F_t$ be the positions not occupied by the observed labels at time $t$. For a specified unobserved label, let $p_t$ be its conditional position law given those complete observed trajectories.*

*The vector $p_t$ evolves from its initial point mass by averaging on pairs with two positions in $F_{t-1}$ and deterministic transport on pairs with one such position. It depends only on the directions and observed paths through time $t$. The uniform vector $1/m$ is transported to the uniform vector on $F_t$. Thus, with $E_t=\sum_{x\in F_t}(p_t(x)-1/m)^2$, one has $0\le E_t\le1$ and <a id="frames:hole-loss"></a>$$E_{t-1}-E_t=\frac12\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset F_{t-1}\text{ paired at }t}
                    (p_{t-1}(x)-p_{t-1}(y))^2.
 \label{frames:hole-loss}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frames-holes"><summary>Proof of stmt-frames-holes</summary>

*Proof.* The online averaging and transport rules are those of Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:energy">7</a>; here we must also justify conditioning on complete observed paths. A feasible specification of those paths prescribes precisely the coins on the visited time-edge pairs. Necessity is immediate. Conversely, those prescribed coins force the paths, by induction over the layers. The path event is therefore a cylinder in the independent coin array. All other coins remain independent and fair, including unvisited coins at earlier times.

On an edge used by an observed label the coin is fixed; on an edge with two unobserved positions it remains fair. Future path constraints use later time-edge pairs and do not alter this earlier recursion. Thus the conditional vector equals the same forward averaging and transport flow obtained from the observed prefix. Applying the exact decrement in Lemma <a href="#shear:lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shear:lem:energy">7</a>, with its available set equal to $F_{t-1}$, proves the displayed identity and transports the uniform comparison vector as stated. ◻

</details>

The next consequence records the two conditioning fields used below. The complete field is convenient for endpoint comparisons; the smaller field is the one in which the next direction is averaged.

<a id="stmt-frames-conditional-interface"></a>
<div id="frames:conditional-interface" class="corollary">

<a id="frames:full-path-prefix"></a>
<a id="frames:direction-factorization"></a>
**Corollary 39** (Complete and chronological conditioning). *Fix integers $T\ge1$ and $1\le q\le n$. Let $\mathbf v=(v_1,\ldots,v_T)$ be a random schedule of nonzero directions. Set $Z_0=I$, and suppose that, conditional on $\mathbf v$, the increments of $Z$ are independent uniform switches in directions $v_1,\ldots,v_T$. Let $C=(c_1,\ldots,c_q)$ be a deterministic list of distinct labels, or a random such list independent of the joint process $(\mathbf v,(Z_t)_{t=0}^T)$. For $1\le j\le q$, put <a id="frames:online-field"></a><a id="frames:terminal-field"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal F_t^{C,j}
 &=\sigma\bigl(C,v_1,\ldots,v_t,\,
       Z_s(c_i):0\le s\le t,\ i<j\bigr), \label{frames:online-field}\\
 \mathcal H^{C,j}
 &=\sigma\bigl(C,\mathbf v,\,
       Z_s(c_i):0\le s\le T,\ i<j\bigr). \label{frames:terminal-field}
\end{aligned}$$ For $0\le t\le T$, define $p_t(x)=\Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x\mid\mathcal F_t^{C,j}\}$. Then, for every $x\in V$, $$\Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x\mid\mathcal H^{C,j}\}=p_t(x)
 \quad\text{almost surely}. \label{frames:full-path-prefix}$$ For $t<T$, let $K_t(v)=\Pr\{v_{t+1}=v\mid v_1,\ldots,v_t\}$. Then $$\Pr\{Z_t(c_j)=x,\ v_{t+1}=v\mid\mathcal F_t^{C,j}\}
 =p_t(x)K_t(v). \label{frames:direction-factorization}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frames-conditional-interface"><summary>Proof of stmt-frames-conditional-interface</summary>

*Proof.* First fix the list, the complete schedule, and feasible complete trajectories of the preceding labels.
